Threshold

Book Four of Working for the Weekend



Chapter 1: Chapter 1



Marie had been staring at the same six years of yield data for three weeks, and the number that finally broke it loose wasn't even a big one.

0.4 percent.

Four-tenths of a percentage point, buried in a column she'd built to catch rounding errors in the Ethiopia reclamation numbers, not revelations. She almost dismissed it as noise the same way she'd dismissed forty other anomalies since she'd started cross-referencing the field data against Home's original baseline archive. Except this one repeated. Same seasonal offset, every single year since the program scaled up, like the land itself had learned a rhythm nobody had actually taught it.

"Cent." She didn't look up from the screen. "Come look at this before I convince myself I'm imagining it."

Cent crossed the workshop in four steps, orbital calculations abandoned mid-equation on the whiteboard behind her — a habit Marie had stopped commenting on months ago, the way half-finished physics just lived on every surface Cent occupied like weather. She leaned over Marie's shoulder, and Marie watched her go very still.

"That's not a measurement artifact," Cent said.

"No."

"That's the land recovering faster than the model predicts it should."

"Faster, and along a curve that matches almost exactly what Home's baseline archive logs as the threshold trajectory — the good one. Stewardship achieved, not collapse averted." Marie sat back, pulling her knees up onto the workshop stool, an old bracing posture Cent recognized from worse nights than this one. "I think what we've been doing out there for six years isn't just good work. I think it's moving the actual needle. Home's own numbers, and we're bending them."

Cent didn't say anything for a moment. Behind her, the whiteboard's equations sat unfinished, deorbit windows and burn calculations for a satellite recovery job that suddenly felt very far away.

"That's good news, though. Isn't it?"

"It should be. I keep waiting for it to feel like good news instead of just—" Marie gestured at the screen, at six years of careful, quiet, deliberately unadvertised work. "Instead of feeling like something that's about to get a lot bigger than the two of us keeping it a secret. Cent, if this trend holds, we're not looking at a decade-long project anymore. We're looking at something that scales. And things that scale get noticed."

*

Two states away, in a workshop that had seen far more late nights than any garage reasonably should, Tony Stark was arguing with a satellite.

"I'm telling you, that's not a sensor artifact," he said, to nobody currently in the room, pulling up the same anomalous imagery for the fourth time that hour — a stretch of reclaimed Ethiopian desert showing a spectral signature that didn't match anything in his materials database, not quite chlorophyll, not quite mineral, sitting there glowing faintly wrong across three separate satellite passes. "FRIDAY, run it again. Compare against every known agricultural tech signature we've got on file."

"Already done, boss. No match. Whatever's out there, it's not on any patent filing, any published research, or any government program I can find a paper trail for."

"That's not comforting."

"It gets less comforting. I cross-referenced the coordinates. Same general region's shown up in three other anomaly flags over the past two years — unexplained yield spikes, soil composition changes that don't track with any known intervention method, and one power signature eighteen months ago that read, briefly, like nothing on Earth."

Tony sat back, the easy deflecting energy draining out of him for a moment, replaced by the particular stillness that meant he'd stopped joking because something had actually gotten his attention.

"Get me Fury," he said. "Tonight, not tomorrow. And pull everything else you've got on unexplained tech signatures over the last five years. I want to know if this is one thing happening in one place, or if I've been ignoring a pattern."

*

Fury took the call standing in a SHIELD situation room that had gone quiet for once, no active crisis on any of the six screens currently glowing around him, which was exactly the kind of calm he'd learned, over a long career, never to fully trust.

"Stark. This better be good, I was almost home."

"Define good. I've got an unexplained tech signature sitting on top of the single most effective agricultural recovery project I've ever seen data on, in a region that had no business producing numbers like this without help nobody's accounting for. And it's not the first flag. It's the fourth."

Fury was already moving toward a terminal before Tony finished the sentence, the stillness of the room evaporating around him.

"Four years," Tony said. "Same general signature pattern, showing up quiet, staying quiet, getting more effective every time it shows up again. Whoever's running this either doesn't know how visible they've become, or doesn't care, and I honestly don't know which possibility worries me more."

"Send me everything," Fury said. "And Stark — keep this off anything that isn't need-to-know for now. If this is what I think it might be, I don't want it getting ahead of us before we understand what we're actually looking at."

"What do you think it might be?"

Fury was quiet for a moment, old instinct working through a shape he hadn't let himself consider seriously in years.

"I think," he said slowly, "we might have just found out somebody's been quietly saving the world for half a decade, and nobody thought to mention it to anybody who could help."

***



Chapter 2: Chapter 2



The knock at the homestead door came three days later, and Cent knew before she'd even opened it that it wasn't the kind of visit they usually got — too formal, too deliberate, a car parked at the exact respectful distance from the house that suggested someone who understood, at least in theory, that they were arriving somewhere they hadn't been invited.

Fury stood on the porch alone, hands visible, the deliberate stillness of a man who'd learned a long time ago that showing up threatening just made everything take longer.

"Cent Rice," he said. It wasn't a question.

"Depends who's asking." Cent didn't move from the doorway. "You're not local."

"No. Name's Fury. I run an organization that spends a lot of time worrying about things that don't officially exist yet." He held up both hands slowly, produced nothing more threatening than a tablet. "I'm not here to make trouble. I'm here because six years of satellite data says your family's been quietly doing something remarkable, and I'd rather have that conversation with you directly than have it happen without you in the room."

Millie appeared behind her in the doorway before she could answer, wiping flour from her hands onto her apron, and went very still at the sight of the tablet, the careful, deliberate stance of the man holding it, the particular weight of a visit she'd apparently been half-expecting for longer than she'd ever said out loud.

"Millie Rice," Fury said, with a small nod, like confirming something he'd already suspected.

"You have my attention," Millie said, quiet, steady, already doing the fast, practiced math of a woman who'd spent her whole marriage learning to read exactly how much danger walked in with a stranger before deciding whether to let them past the threshold. "Show me what you think you know, and then we'll decide together whether you're someone I want inside this house."

Fury turned the tablet around.

Satellite imagery filled the screen — the Ethiopia site, the spectral anomaly glowing faint and strange across the reclaimed acreage, three other flagged locations pulled up beside it in smaller frames, years of data compressed into a handful of damning, beautiful images.

"Four years of unexplained signatures," Fury said. "All tracking back, eventually, to intervention work nobody's ever publicly claimed credit for. I had a man spend three days confirming there's no government program, no corporate initiative, no academic study that accounts for any of it. Just results. The kind of results that shouldn't be possible, coming from somewhere nobody's been looking."

Millie studied the screen for a long moment, something complicated moving behind her eyes — not quite fear, not quite relief, the look of someone finally setting down a weight she'd been carrying alone for longer than was reasonable.

"How much do you actually know," she said, "and how much is guessing dressed up as data."

"Enough to know it's not natural. Not enough to know what it actually is, where it comes from, or who else might already be looking at the exact same anomalies I am." Fury's voice stayed level, careful. "That last part's the reason I came here quietly, alone, instead of with a full team and a warrant I don't currently have grounds to get. If I found this, somebody else eventually will too. I'd rather you hear that from someone willing to have an honest conversation about it first."

Davy appeared behind Millie, wary, protective, the old careful stillness Charlie recognized from every conversation that ever touched too close to the family's actual history.

"And if we don't want to have that conversation," Davy said.

"Then I walk away, and I hope whoever finds this next is as steady as I'm trying to be." Fury met his eyes without flinching. "But I don't think that's actually the choice in front of you. I think the choice is who you have this conversation with, not whether it happens. And for what it's worth — six years of data, and not one flag I found suggests you've ever used any of this for anything but exactly what it looks like. Helping people. That matters to me. It's why I'm standing here asking instead of standing somewhere else giving orders."

Millie looked at Davy, some silent, decades-practiced conversation passing between them, and then back at Fury, weighing something Cent couldn't fully read from where she stood.

"Come in," she said finally. "But you're explaining exactly how much you actually know, in full, before I explain anything at all. I've spent too many years protecting this family to start trusting a stranger's tablet over my own judgment now."

"Fair," Fury said, and meant it, stepping through the door into a kitchen that smelled like bread and was about to become a great deal more complicated than either of them expected.

***



Chapter 3: Chapter 3



Marie's phone rang forty minutes after Fury walked through the Rice family's front door, and Cent watched her go pale reading the caller ID before she'd even answered it.

"That's Millie's number," Marie said, hand hovering over the screen. "She never calls during work hours."

She answered, put it on speaker without being asked, and Millie's voice came through tight and controlled in the way Cent had learned, over years of knowing her, meant something serious enough that she'd already decided panic wasn't an option she could afford to indulge.

"Somebody found us," Millie said, without preamble. "A man named Fury. Says he runs some kind of organization. He's got satellite data going back four years, tracking the reclamation work straight to us. He's sitting in my kitchen right now, and I need to know exactly what you two have already put together on your end, because I have a feeling it's about to matter a great deal."

Marie and Cent looked at each other, the same thought arriving in both of them at once.

"We found something too," Marie said. "This week. The yield data's not just good, Millie. It's bending Home's own baseline trajectory. We think the work's actually moving the needle on the stewardship threshold, not just helping locally."

Silence on the other end, long enough that Cent wondered if the call had dropped.

"Say that again," Millie said finally, quieter.

"The intervention work — Ethiopia, the other sites — it's tracking along the favorable curve. The one that matters. If it holds, this isn't charity work anymore. It's the actual thing Home's mission was originally supposed to be watching for, except we're not watching for it. We're making it happen."

Another silence, and when Millie spoke again, something in her voice had shifted — steadier, sharper, a woman recalculating the entire shape of a threat in real time and finding it, unexpectedly, less purely dangerous than she'd braced for.

"That changes what kind of conversation I'm about to have with this man," she said. "Marie, Cent — I want you both here. Today, if you can manage it. If what we've been doing actually matters this much, I don't want to be the only one deciding how to explain it to someone with this much reach."

"We're on our way," Cent said, already reaching for the truck keys, Marie a step behind her, already on the phone confirming the Maule had fuel.

They made the hangar in twenty minutes, and Marie had the plane rolled out and running through her checklist by the time Cent finished throwing both their laptops in the back. Neither of them talked much on the flight — through the gate, tame-side afternoon giving way to wildside grassland the instant the wheels crossed the threshold, then on to the strip closest to the fence line, the one Davy and Charlie's father had graded together two summers back specifically so a trip like this one wouldn't have to end in an eight-mile hike anymore. Davy was already waiting with the truck when they touched down, and the last stretch into the homestead itself passed in tense, hurried quiet.

They arrived at the homestead two hours after the call, all told, to find the kitchen transformed into something between a family meeting and a briefing room, Fury seated at the table with a level of stillness that made the whole room feel more serious just by his presence in it, Davy standing near the window with his arms crossed, Charlie hovering somewhere between curious and protective.

"Marie Chen. Cent Rice," Fury said, before either of them had a chance to introduce themselves, a small file already open on his tablet. "I did some homework on the drive here. You two are the reason the numbers I found actually make sense instead of just being unexplainable."

"We just cross-reference data," Marie said, cautious. "We didn't build anything you'd call weapons-grade, if that's what you're worried about."

"That's not remotely what I'm worried about." Fury set the tablet down. "I've spent six years watching organizations try to solve problems at the scale you people have quietly solved without any of them coming close. What I'm trying to figure out is whether what you've built is something the world needs protected, amplified, and taken seriously at an institutional level — or whether I've just found the single easiest thing for the wrong people to try to take from you once they realize what it's worth."

Millie's hand found Davy's across the room, the old, practiced gesture of two people bracing together for whatever came next.

"That's the actual question, isn't it," she said. "Not whether you found us. Whether finding us puts a target on everything we've spent years quietly building."

"That's exactly the question," Fury agreed. "And I don't think either of us gets to answer it alone. Which is why, before this goes any further, I want to bring in people who can actually help protect this properly if it comes to that — not just SHIELD assets, people who understand what it means to have power that outstrips the world's readiness for it, and who've made the choice to use it carefully anyway."

"Who," Davy asked.

Something close to a small, tired smile crossed Fury's face.

"A god who's spent centuries thinking about exactly this kind of responsibility. A king whose entire country's built around protecting resources the rest of the world would exploit given half a chance. A scientist who understands better than almost anyone what it costs to carry something the world isn't ready to see yet." He looked around the kitchen, at the family gathered there, at six years of quiet, remarkable work nobody outside this room had ever properly witnessed. "I think it's time this stopped being a secret you're carrying alone. Not because I want to expose you. Because I think you've earned better backup than a satellite anomaly and a worried government agent."

***



Chapter 4: Chapter 4



"Home didn't build the gate," Millie said, and the sentence landed in the room with a weight that made Cent realize, despite a lifetime of hearing bits and pieces of her mother's history, that she'd never actually heard her say it plainly before. "I don't think I ever told any of you that outright, because I didn't think it mattered — Home's who I grew up with, who taught me what I know, who I've spent my whole adult life trying to represent honestly out here. But the gate itself, the actual mechanism — that's older than Home. Home found it. Home's spent generations studying it, maintaining a piece of it, deciding who gets to walk through the piece they control. But they didn't make it, and they didn't write the mission's actual terms. Somebody else did."

"Then who did," Davy said.

"I don't know their name. I only ever knew the word Home uses for them, which isn't really a name so much as a description — the closest translation's something like *the keepers*, though that flattens a lot of nuance the actual word carries. Home's relationship with them isn't close. It's not hostile, as far as I ever understood growing up, but it's not warm either. More like — a landlord who occasionally shows up to check the property's being maintained to standard, and otherwise leaves you entirely alone to run your section of it. They're the ones who set the two conditions. Stewardship, or collapse. Watch and wait, either way, until one of them's actually met."

"But we didn't wait," Cent said, quiet, working it through out loud. "We didn't watch for stewardship to happen on its own. We went out and built it. That's not the same thing as what the mission actually says to do."

The room went still at that, the particular stillness of a question nobody had quite let themselves ask directly until now.

"So the actual problem," Marie said slowly, "isn't that we did something wrong. It's that we did something nobody ever gave us permission to do, and we don't know if the people who actually wrote the rules are going to see it as a solution or as somebody jumping the fence they were supposed to be watching from a distance."

"That's exactly the problem," Millie said. "I don't know how the Keepers judge intervention versus emergence. I don't know if there's even a meaningful difference to them between an outcome that happens naturally and one that gets pushed into being by six people with a truck and a lot of stubbornness. I never had cause to ask, because nobody in my family ever tried what you two just told me you've actually pulled off."

"So we might be in trouble," Charlie said, "for doing exactly the thing the mission was supposedly hoping somebody would eventually do."

"You might be. Or you might be about to become the first proof in the mission's entire history that the favorable outcome is actually achievable, which is either going to make Home celebrate you as the reason this whole project finally means something, or terrify them that a family acted unilaterally on a decision that was never supposed to be any single group's to make alone." Millie's hand found Davy's, the old, practiced gesture of two people bracing together. "I honestly don't know which reaction we're about to get. I don't think anyone in this room does."

Steve had said little since the meeting started, but he broke his silence now, choosing the pause carefully. "For what it's worth, that's not actually a bad position to be arguing from. You didn't hide anything malicious. You solved a problem the people watching for it apparently couldn't solve themselves. That's a strong case, whatever the reaction turns out to be."

"It's a strong case if anyone's willing to actually hear it that way," Natasha said, practical, already several steps ahead. "Institutions don't always reward the people who solve their problems without asking permission first. Sometimes they punish the precedent instead, regardless of the outcome."

Fury, who'd been listening with the particular stillness of a man weighing exactly how much to say, finally spoke.

"Which is why the timing of this next part matters," he said. "Home reached out to me directly, three days ago. Before any of this conversation happened. Wanted a meeting — Council-level, formal request, no stated agenda beyond 'matters concerning Designated Earth 7-Cascade requiring joint discussion.'" His expression didn't shift, but something in his voice did, the careful weight of a man who'd been sitting on a piece of the puzzle the whole afternoon, waiting for the room to catch up enough to need it. "I was planning to mention it eventually. Didn't expect it to turn out to be quite this relevant, quite this fast. But if Home's asking for a formal Council sit-down the exact week your own data confirms the trajectory's actually bending—"

"That's not a coincidence," Natasha said.

"No," Fury agreed. "I don't believe it is. I think Home's own instruments found the exact same thing yours did, and I think they're every bit as uncertain about what to do with it as you are."

***



Chapter 5: Chapter 5



They flew back to Sunnydale that night instead of staying, Faith's call more than his — "everybody needs a room that isn't this one for a few hours, Xan, us included" — and Xander hadn't argued, mostly because she wasn't wrong.

The house was quiet when they got in, the particular late-night quiet of a home that had learned, over two years, to hold a lot of noise most days and none at all on the nights that actually needed it. Faith kicked her boots off by the door, dropped onto the couch without turning on more than one lamp, and Xander caught himself standing in his own kitchen for a solid minute before he registered he'd walked there at all.

"You're doing the thing," Faith called, not looking up.

"What thing."

"The thing where something enormous happens and you go make tea like it's a normal Tuesday because your brain needs your hands busy before it'll let the rest of you catch up."

He looked down at the kettle, already filled, already in his hand, and had to admit she had a point.

"In my defense," he said, "it kind of is a normal Tuesday. That's the whole horrifying thing about it. Somebody's been quietly running a planet-scale rescue operation for six years, all by themselves, no backup, no permission slip, and Tony Stark's satellites caught them before Home even fully understood what their own numbers were showing. There wasn't a dramatic reveal moment. There wasn't music. Marie just went 'huh' and then everyone's life got a lot more complicated."

Faith was quiet for a second, the kind of quiet that meant she was choosing her next words instead of just reaching for the first ones.

"You good?" she said, finally.

"Working on it." He set the kettle on the burner, more out of ritual than actual thirst at this point. "Keeps making me think about my own jumping, honestly. Four years I did that alone, nobody knowing, nobody watching, and it never once occurred to me that keeping it quiet might eventually become its own kind of problem — not because I was doing anything wrong, just because eventually somebody notices, and then it's not your secret to keep anymore. Davy's family just found that out the hard way, at a much bigger scale than I ever had to."

"That's a scary kind of found out. Not in trouble, exactly. Just — suddenly not private anymore, and not sure yet whether that's good or bad."

"Yeah." He leaned against the counter, watching her in the low lamp light, the particular stillness she carried on nights when the day had actually gotten to him. "Makes me think about what happens if I ever get found the same way. If somebody with a satellite decides my jumping's worth flagging too."

"You'd have people in your corner already, if that happened. That's kind of the whole point of tonight, right? Nobody's facing this alone anymore. Not them, not you either, if it ever comes to that."

He didn't say anything for a moment, the kettle starting its low building hiss behind him.

"When'd you get so wise," he said, eventually, the old deflection, offered soft instead of sharp.

"Told you. I have my moments." She patted the couch beside her, an invitation more than a command. "C'mere. Kettle can scream at nobody for a few minutes. I want you closer than the kitchen."

He came, because he always did, and the kettle did scream at nobody for a while, and neither of them minded.

*

Cent found her mother on the homestead porch a little after midnight, the rest of the house finally gone quiet, Davy asleep on the couch in the particular collapsed way of a man who'd spent the whole day holding something too heavy to keep holding once nobody needed him upright anymore.

"You should be resting too," Millie said, not turning.

"So should you." Cent sat beside her, the old porch boards creaking the way they always did under the second person's weight. "Keep thinking about what you said. About the Keepers not caring whether the good outcome happens naturally or gets pushed into being. Six years of work, and I never once considered that succeeding might be its own kind of risk."

"I never considered it either. That's the part that scares me more than getting caught, if I'm honest." Millie's voice had gone thin, tired in a way that had nothing to do with the hour. "I spent your whole life teaching you to be careful about being seen, about being found — never once occurred to me the actual danger might come from succeeding too well at the thing we were quietly trying to do all along."

"We didn't do anything wrong, though," Cent said, firm, the same steady certainty she brought to orbital math and family arguments alike. "We helped people. Real people, real land, real results. Whatever the Keepers decide about how we got there, that part doesn't stop being true."

Millie reached over, found her daughter's hand in the dark, and held on.

"When did you get so sure of things," she said, something almost like a laugh underneath it.

"Learned it from you. Don't tell me otherwise. I was there for all of it."

They lingered outside, the compound quiet around them, somewhere off past the strip a coyote calling once and going silent. Cent thought, not for the first time this strange, enormous day, that whatever came next — Council meetings, Keepers, a judgment neither of them could predict yet — this part, at least, the sitting quiet with her mother's hand in hers under a sky that had never asked permission to hold this many stars, wasn't going anywhere. Whatever else the day had cost them, it hadn't cost them this.

***



Chapter 6: Chapter 6



Charlie's father had the Cessna out at first light, running preflight with the same unhurried thoroughness he brought to everything mechanical, like the airplane deserved the same respect he'd give a person he was about to trust with his life.

"Council meeting's not till tomorrow," Charlie said, holding the fuel sample cup while his father checked it for water and sediment against the pale morning sky. "We got a run to make today too?"

"Two, if the weather holds." His father tilted the cup, satisfied, poured it back. "Davy wants the second strip's grading finished before anybody official shows up here — man's not wrong that it looks better having two working runways than one, if we're about to have Home's whole Council standing around judging how serious we are about this place. And I want the fuel cached at the far strip before we're all too distracted with diplomacy to remember logistics matter regardless of how enormous the week's gotten."

They loaded the plane methodically — fifty-five-gallon drums lashed forward of the CG limit, weight and balance worked out on a scrap of paper his father kept folded in his shirt pocket, the same one he'd used every trip since the strip first went in. "Density altitude's going to bite us if we're not careful," his father said, checking the thermometer nailed to the hangar post. "Eighty-one degrees, five thousand two hundred field elevation — that's pushing seven thousand five hundred effective by the time we're loaded and it's properly warmed up. Cessna's going to want every foot of that runway and then some."

"Still good margins, though."

"Good margins if we respect them. Bad ones if we get casual because we've done this forty times already." His father climbed in, ran the checklist out loud from habit more than necessity at this point. "Fuel selector, both. Mixture, rich for the density altitude — don't lean it till we're established in the climb. Flaps, ten degrees for the short field. Trim set." He glanced over. "You want the right seat today, or you want to sit this one out and keep an eye on the ground crew?"

"Right seat," Charlie said, without hesitation, and climbed in.

The takeoff roll ate more runway than it would have on a cold morning, exactly as his father had predicted, the Cessna hauling itself off the graded dirt with the particular reluctant heaviness of an airplane doing plain work instead of just flying itself around empty. Charlie watched the airspeed indicator climb through the numbers his father called out loud — "Rotate's coming up around fifty-five today, little later than usual, that's the density altitude talking" — and felt the quiet satisfaction of watching a system work exactly the way math said it should, no surprises, no margin eaten by carelessness.

They leveled off at pattern altitude for the short hop to the far strip, grass and empty country rolling out gold beneath them in every direction, and Charlie realized he was thinking, not for the first time, that there was something almost unbearably ordinary about mornings like this — fuel drums and density altitude and a father teaching a son the same careful math he'd learned from his own father — sitting right up against something as enormous as satellite anomalies and interdimensional politics and a Council meeting that might reshape everything both families thought they understood about the door in the barn.

"You thinking about tomorrow," his father said, not really a question.

"Some."

"Good to think about it. Better not to think about nothing else." His father banked the plane gently toward the second strip, already visible as a pale scar through the grass ahead. "Whatever happens at that meeting, this—" he tipped his head, taking in the plane, the cargo, the ordinary work of the morning, "—this doesn't stop mattering. Families still need fuel cached. Strips still need grading. World doesn't pause just because the politics got enormous."

"Feels weird, focusing on runway grading with everything else going on."

"Feels weird because it is weird. Doesn't make it wrong." His father set the plane down clean, the wheels finding packed earth with barely a shudder, taxiing toward the cache point with the same easy confidence he brought to every landing on ground he'd helped build himself. "Your Uncle Max understood that, best I ever heard. Kept watching this land right up until the day he died out there, still waiting on a mission that never did resolve clean while he was alive to see it. Didn't mean the watching didn't matter. Meant he understood what mattered enough to keep at it, even without ever getting the answer himself."

They unloaded in comfortable quiet, drums rolled off and staged under the tarp at the strip's edge, and Charlie noticed he was, mid-task, thinking about his great-uncle in a way he hadn't quite let himself before — not as a name attached to an old grief and an empty canteen, but as a man who'd woken up most mornings and done ordinary, careful work on land he loved, right up until the world he was watching finally took more from him than it gave back.

*

Back at the homestead, the Council meeting logistics had taken over most of the main room, Millie and Fury bent over a rough seating chart while Willow cross-referenced diplomatic protocol against three different fragmentary sources with the dogged determination of someone who refused to walk into any room, interdimensional or otherwise, underprepared.

"Home's sending a full delegation," Millie was saying, when Charlie came in dust-streaked and smelling of avgas. "Not just the usual liaison. That alone tells me this is being treated as more serious than a routine check-in."

"Any word on whether the Keepers are sending anyone," Cent asked, from the corner, where she'd been quietly running the trajectory numbers again, still not quite believing them.

"None. And I don't know if that's reassuring or the opposite." Millie rubbed her eyes, the accumulated exhaustion of the last two days finally showing through the careful composure she'd been holding for everyone else's benefit. "If they're not coming, maybe this stays a manageable conversation with people we actually understand. If they are coming, and just haven't said so—"

"Then we should assume they're already watching whatever we do to prepare," Natasha finished, flat, practical. "Which means the actual meeting isn't tomorrow. It started the moment Marie's numbers confirmed the trajectory was unmistakable."

The room went quiet at that, the kind of quiet that settles over a truth nobody particularly wanted but everybody recognized as accurate the moment it was said out loud.

"Well," Tony said, into the silence, not quite managing to keep the edge of genuine unease out of his usual deflecting brightness, "good thing we're all on our best behavior, then."

***



Chapter 7: Chapter 7



They came through the gate at exactly the hour they'd promised, which Millie noted quietly to Davy as somehow more unsettling than if they'd been late — Home rarely managed that kind of precision without wanting it noticed.

Four delegates, not one liaison this time. Charlie recognized none of them, though his mother's posture shifted subtly at the sight of the woman leading them, something between old respect and old wariness settling into her shoulders.

"Councilor Ashgrove," Millie said, formal in a way Charlie had rarely heard from her. "Wasn't expecting you personally."

"Wasn't expecting to need to come personally," the woman said. Older than Millie, silver-threaded dark hair pulled back severe and practical, dressed in something between a uniform and ordinary clothes that seemed engineered to communicate authority without quite announcing it. Her eyes swept the assembled room — Avengers and family alike — with the unhurried thoroughness of someone taking inventory rather than being impressed. "You've assembled quite a gathering, Millie Rice. I'd heard rumors. Hadn't quite credited how literally accurate they were."

"Circumstances warranted it."

"So I understand." Ashgrove's gaze found the whiteboard, still covered in Marie's careful math, the trajectory curve plotted out in careful, unhurried handwriting. Something crossed her face — not quite alarm, something closer to true astonishment held carefully in check. "You've confirmed it, then. The trend's honest."

The room went very still.

"You already knew," Davy said, low, guarded in a way Charlie recognized from every conversation that had ever touched too close to this family being watched by people they hadn't chosen. "You knew, and you let us find out on our own from a satellite anomaly instead of telling us directly."

"I suspected," Ashgrove said, sharp enough to cut through the accusation without raising her own voice, "for perhaps eight months. I did not have confirmation, and I did not have the standing to bring this to full Council attention on suspicion alone — not without risking exactly the kind of premature exposure that's now happened anyway, courtesy of a very observant piece of human technology I confess none of us properly accounted for." She let that settle. "I should have found a way to tell you regardless. I judged the risk of being wrong more dangerous than the risk of staying quiet. I see now that judgment cost you time you should have had to prepare for this on your own terms."

Steve stepped forward slightly, the careful, measured motion of a man positioning himself without quite making it look like positioning.

"Perhaps we start," he said, "with what you can actually tell us. Officially. On record."

Ashgrove studied him a long moment — soldier recognizing soldier, Charlie thought, some old professional respect passing between them that didn't need explaining.

"Fairly asked," she said, and took the seat Millie offered her, the other three delegates arranging themselves silent and watchful behind her. "Then let me tell you what Home actually knows, and what we've only ever suspected, and I'll be plain about which is which, because I think you've all earned better than diplomatic hedging today."

She folded her hands.

"The gate technology was not built by Home. We inherited stewardship of a small portion of it, generations ago, under terms none of us alive today negotiated and few of us fully understand. The mission's actual terms — the two conditions, stewardship or collapse — were set by the Keepers, not by us. What I do know is this: the Keepers do not act as one body. There are factions. There have always been factions. Most of what Home has ever dealt with is the moderate center — cautious, procedural, more concerned with maintaining balance across the doors they oversee than with any single Earth's fate."

"And what do the factions think about what we did," Millie asked. "Taking the mission into our own hands instead of just watching."

Ashgrove's expression tightened, weighing the honesty of her answer before giving it.

"That's precisely what's unresolved, and precisely why I came personally rather than sending a routine liaison. There is a faction — cautious, procedural, deeply invested in the mission staying exactly as originally written — who may see unilateral intervention as a violation of the very neutrality the mission depends on, regardless of the outcome it produced. And there is another faction who will see six years of results as the single strongest argument in the mission's entire history that the favorable condition isn't just theoretically possible, but achievable, on a timeline Home never dared hope for." She looked around the room. "I don't yet know which argument wins. I suspect, honestly, that both are about to be made forcefully, by people who've been waiting a very long time for a reason to have this particular fight."

"So we might get punished for succeeding," Cent said, quiet, working it through.

"Or celebrated for it. Possibly both, from different directions, before this resolves." Ashgrove's jaw tightened. "That should concern you as much as it might reassure you. It means there is no single verdict coming. Only an honest disagreement, playing out at a level none of you have ever had to navigate directly before now."

Fury, silent through most of it, finally spoke.

"And what happens," he said, "if the faction that doesn't like what this family did decides to make an example out of them."

Ashgrove met his eyes without flinching.

"Then Home will do everything within our authority to prevent that outcome," she said. "I will not pretend that authority is absolute. I will not pretend I can guarantee it. But I came here myself, today, specifically because I intend to argue this family's case personally, for as long as I am able to matter in the effort." She looked around the room — Avengers, family, the whole strange assembled shape of it — and something almost like relief crossed her face. "And I confess, seeing what's gathered here today, I feel a good deal better about the odds of that argument succeeding than I did walking through that gate an hour ago."

***



Chapter 8: Chapter 8



"If we're calling this what it actually is," Fury said, once Ashgrove and her delegation had withdrawn to the porch to give the room space to talk freely, "then we're not looking at a family problem anymore. We're looking at a family that just became a test case for an interdimensional institution deciding, in real time, whether unilateral good works get protected or punished. That's not a two-agent problem. That's an all-hands problem, whichever way it breaks."

"You're thinking full roster," Natasha said.

"I'm thinking full roster." Fury was already reaching for his phone. "Thor's off-world, but off-world's exactly the kind of distance that stops mattering when the threat's interdimensional to begin with — man's dealt with more cosmic bureaucracy than the rest of us combined, and honestly, having an actual god in the room tends to focus everyone else's diplomacy wonderfully. Banner's stateside, and if we end up needing to understand technology none of us recognize, there's nobody better. Barton's two hours out. And Wakanda's got more experience defending against exactly this kind of outside-power overreach than anyone else on the planet — T'Challa's going to want to be part of this conversation regardless of whether we ask, so we might as well ask."

"And Giles," Xander said, quiet, the first thing he'd said since Ashgrove's revelation had sunk fully into the room. Everyone looked at him. "I know he's not exactly Avengers-caliber muscle. But he's spent thirty years being the guy who actually reads the old texts nobody else wants to touch, and if there's precedent anywhere — old Watcher records, anything about doors between worlds, anything about how outside powers have judged people like us before — he's the one who'll know where to look. Ashgrove's talking about factions and precedent like it's settled law somewhere. I want somebody on our side who actually knows how to read that kind of law."

"Good instinct," Fury said, already dialing. "Make the call."

Ashgrove, listening with the careful attention of someone cataloguing everything for a report she hadn't written yet, glanced at Millie. "This 'full roster' — I confess Home's own briefings on your world's defenders are somewhat dated. I wasn't aware the original team was still so completely intact."

"We got lucky," Fury said, flat, in the specific tone of a man who didn't examine that luck too closely for fear of jinxing it. "Whatever crisis your world's version of us usually gets tested by, ours never came. No cosmic invasion, no snap, no team torn apart trying to survive it. I don't know why. I've stopped asking. I just make sure we don't waste it."

"A fortunate divergence," Ashgrove said, something thoughtful in her voice.

"The most fortunate one I know of," Fury agreed, and left it there, already moving on to the next call.

*

They arrived over the following six hours in a scattered, mismatched procession that would have looked, from any ordinary vantage point, like the setup for a joke nobody had gotten to the punchline of yet — a god, a scientist, an archer, a king, and a librarian, converging on a homestead barn in rural nowhere, most of them arriving through methods the Rice family's fence posts had never remotely been built to expect.

Thor came through first, and not subtly — a crack of light and ozone at the edge of the strip that had Bessie-Two bolting for the far paddock and Charlie's father muttering something about needing a longer conversation regarding advance warning next time. Thor took in the assembled crowd, the whiteboard, the tension still hanging thick in the room, and set down the strange, elaborately wrapped item he'd been carrying with the easy confidence of a man who understood exactly how odd he looked doing it.

"I brought mead," he announced. "It seemed the sort of occasion that called for it, once Fury explained the shape of things. Also, I have some small experience with bureaucratic factions claiming authority over more than they've earned. I suspect I may be useful for more than the mead."

"We'll take both," Millie said, and meant it.

Bruce arrived quieter, driven up the last stretch by a SHIELD car that had clearly taken the unpaved road slower than strictly necessary, folder of borrowed equipment already under one arm before he'd fully gotten out. He took one look at Marie's trajectory data still glowing on the screen, and was crouched beside her within thirty seconds, the two of them falling into an easy technical shorthand that left most of the room several steps behind within a single exchange.

Clint came in low-key, the unhurried competence of a man who'd learned a long time ago that the loud entrances belonged to other people, nodded once to Natasha, once to Fury, and found a spot near the door that put eyes on every exit without anyone particularly noticing he'd done it.

T'Challa arrived last, through a means far more understated than Thor's — a quiet vehicle, an even quieter escort, and a bearing that filled the room's remaining tension with something that felt, oddly, like reassurance rather than additional weight.

"Councilor," he said to Ashgrove, on the porch, with the careful formal courtesy of one head of state acknowledging another, "Wakanda has some history with outside powers who believe our resources exist for their evaluation rather than our own stewardship, and a long history of arguing that a nation's — or a family's — right to use what they've built as they see fit shouldn't depend on someone else's permission after the fact. I don't say that to claim we understand your Keepers fully. I say it because I suspect this family could use someone in the room who's made that exact argument before, successfully, to people who very much did not want to hear it."

"I would welcome that perspective," Ashgrove said, and meant it plainly enough that even Davy, still tense and watchful in the corner, looked briefly, plainly comforted.

Giles came through the gate nearly two hours after the others, having apparently spent the drive to the airfield and the flight itself buried in three separate texts he'd grabbed off his own shelves the moment Xander's call came through, and walked into the crowded main room already mid-sentence.

"—and if the notion of an outside power judging a mortal family's unsanctioned good deeds sounds at all familiar, which I rather suspect it will once I've had a proper look, there's a reasonable chance the Watchers' own archives touch on something similar, several centuries back, in material nobody's had cause to properly cross-reference before now, because frankly nobody's ever had a case quite like this one to compare it against." He looked up, seeming to register the size of the room for the first time. "Ah. Hello, everyone. Sorry — I do get like this. Where's the data? I should like to see the actual trajectory numbers before I say anything else with false confidence."

Willow handed him the laptop without a word, and he dropped into the nearest chair with the immediate, total focus of a man who'd found, after thirty years, a problem truly worthy of it.

The room had, within a handful of hours, become something Charlie didn't quite have a word for yet — not a war council, not exactly a family gathering, some strange, crowded overlap of both, gods and scientists and kings and librarians and two ordinary families who'd started this whole enormous thing with nothing but a flag on a fence post and a summer that had refused, at every turn, to stay simple.

"Alright," Fury said, once the last of the introductions had wrapped up and the room's attention had gathered, heavy and ready, toward the center of it. "Let's find out exactly what we're dealing with."

***



Chapter 9: Chapter 9



Giles worked the way he always worked when a text actually mattered — slowly, methodically, three books open at once and a fourth balanced precariously on his knee, muttering half-formed connections under his breath that nobody else in the room was meant to hear yet.

"The phonetic root," he said, eventually, loud enough to be addressed to the room rather than himself, "matches — not exactly, but closely enough I don't think it's coincidence — a designation that appears exactly twice in the entire Watchers' Council archive. Both references are old. Pre-dating any Slayer line record I've ever cross-checked, which for context means far older than anything else in those archives, and those archives go back a very long way."

"What's it say," Cent asked.

"Frustratingly little in the first instance, and rather more in the second." Giles adjusted his glasses, the old habit Xander had watched a hundred times across a hundred crises. "The first reference is a single line, buried in a section cataloguing what the original scribe calls 'doors not of our making.' It reads, roughly translated: *the keepers judge intent as harshly as outcome, and mercy is rarely their instrument.* No further elaboration. Unsettling on its own, but not yet useful."

"And the second," Millie said.

"The second is a good deal more relevant." Giles turned the book around, though the text meant nothing to anyone who couldn't read it, the gesture more habit than communication. "A brief account of an incident the scribe describes only as 'the reckoning of the Coelfrith line' — a family, it seems, who maintained some manner of doorway or threshold, not unlike your own, somewhere in what I'd guess was medieval Northumbria given the naming convention. The account states that the family, without sanction, began using access through their door to relieve a famine spreading through the surrounding lands — grain, seed stock, knowledge of storage methods, all drawn from beyond the threshold and given freely. Within a generation, the region's fortunes had truly turned. And within that same generation, 'those who judge such things' arrived to determine whether the family had served the greater design, or simply imposed their own will upon it."

"What'd they decide," Cent asked, quiet.

"That's the frustrating part. The scribe doesn't say, plainly — only that the Coelfrith line 'endured judgment, and were changed by it, though the doorway itself remained theirs to keep.' Which reads, to me, less like punishment and more like scrutiny survived. A test passed, at some cost the record doesn't specify, rather than a family destroyed for their initiative."

The room had gone very quiet.

"That's actually hopeful," Natasha said, careful, testing the shape of the word. "If that's the actual precedent. A family acts without permission, does plain good, gets tested rather than punished, and keeps what they built."

"I'd caution against too much optimism from a single medieval account translated through at least two languages I'm not entirely confident in," Giles said. "But it is, at minimum, evidence that 'endured without sanction' is not automatically synonymous with 'condemned for it.' The Keepers apparently distinguish between intervention that serves their actual aims and intervention that merely resembles it. That distinction is going to matter enormously for how Home's Council frames what's happened here."

"Which means the actual fight," Steve said, working it through out loud, "isn't going to be about whether you broke a rule. It's going to be about whether what you built proves the mission's unmistakable aim, or just imitates it well enough to complicate their judgment."

"That tracks with what Ashgrove said," T'Challa added. "Two factions arguing, not because the facts are in dispute, but because the interpretation is. History suggests that argument can be survived. It does not suggest it will be comfortable."

"Small comfort," Davy muttered, "knowing our best case is 'endured judgment and were changed by it.'"

"I'll take small comfort over none," Millie said, quiet, her hand finding his again.

Bruce hadn't said much, working steadily alongside Willow at a second laptop, but he looked up now with the particular expression of a man who'd found something and wasn't sure yet whether it was good news.

"I think I can build something useful out of this regardless of which way the judgment goes," he said. "Not a weapon — I want to be clear about that going in, because I don't think that's what this situation needs and I don't think it's what any of you would want regardless. A monitor. Something that reads for the same resonance signature Marie's already isolated in the trajectory data, but continuously, in real time, instead of retroactively. If the Keepers are actively evaluating this Earth right now, the way Ashgrove's account suggests, we should be able to tell the difference between routine observation and an actual verdict forming — a spike, a pattern shift, something we can see coming instead of finding out after the fact."

"How long," Fury asked.

"With Marie's baseline model, Giles's translation work, and Stark's equipment once he catches up with us tomorrow—" Bruce did a quick mental accounting, "—three days. Maybe four, if we want it reliable instead of just fast."

"Do it," Millie said, immediately, no hesitation. "Whatever you need from us, take it. I've spent six years not knowing whether the work actually mattered to anyone watching. I'd like to actually see the verdict coming, for once, instead of just waiting on it blind."

Thor, who had been quiet for a stretch uncharacteristic of him, set down the mug he'd been nursing and spoke into the settling silence.

"I have stood before powers who believed their judgment final, their authority beyond question," he said. "I will tell you honestly what experience has taught me about them, because I think this family has earned honesty over comfortable reassurance today." He looked around the room, meeting each face in turn. "Judgment of this kind is rarely as fixed as it presents itself. A power that sends factions to argue rather than simply issuing a verdict is a power still capable of being persuaded. You have already done the one thing that matters most in such a case, simply by having genuine results to show for six years of work, simply by refusing to let this become a quiet, comfortable secret any longer." He raised the mug slightly, an old gesture that needed no translation. "Whatever comes next, you do not face that judgment as one family standing alone at a door. You face it as this."

Nobody said anything for a moment, the weight of it settling easy and solid over a room that had spent the entire day accumulating uncertainty and hope in roughly equal measure.

"Well," Tony said, finally, arriving through the door a beat late with an armful of equipment cases and impeccably terrible timing, "glad I didn't miss the inspirational speech. Somebody catch me up on the whole cosmic-judgment thing while I set up, I work faster with context."

***



Chapter 10: Chapter 10



Buffy found Willow exactly where she expected to — hunched over three separate screens in the Magic Box's back room, the shop closed for the day, a whiteboard already half-filled with notation that had started, at some point in the last hour, to look a lot more like battle planning than research.

"You've been at this since we got back," Buffy said, setting down two coffees neither of them had asked for but both of them needed. "It's been six hours."

"Six productive hours." Willow didn't look up. "I've cross-referenced everything Giles pulled about the Coelfrith account against every threshold-family reference in the Watcher archive I can access remotely. There's not much. Three more mentions, all fragmentary, all consistent with the same basic shape — families who did something with borrowed power that mattered, and then got evaluated for it afterward."

"Any of them end badly?"

"One, maybe. The record's too damaged to be sure. The other two read more like the Coelfrith account — hard, but survivable." Willow finally looked up, and Buffy caught, for the first time all day, how tired she actually was underneath the focus. "I keep thinking about how this connects to Xander. His ability traces back to the actual first Slayer, we know that much. If the Keepers are the ones who set terms for doors and thresholds generally, not just Home's specific gate — does that mean Xander's ability falls under their judgment too? Has it always?"

Buffy went still a moment, feeling the shape of a worry she hadn't quite let herself have yet.

"I don't know," she admitted. "I don't think Xander's thought about it that way either. Four years of solo jumping, and it never occurred to any of us that somebody might be grading it."

"Maybe nobody was. Maybe it's specific to actual doors, actual thresholds, not whatever mechanism gives Xander his ability." Willow rubbed her eyes. "Or maybe we've just been lucky that nobody with standing to judge it ever had reason to look closely. I don't love either possibility, honestly."

"What do you need from me? Actually need, not just moral support, though you've got that too."

Willow considered the question seriously, the way she considered most things that mattered.

"Company, mostly. And maybe — I don't know, an actual conversation about whether we tell the roster about Xander's ability specifically, or whether we let this stay about the Rice family's situation for now. Because if the Keepers really do judge intervention broadly, not just gate-specific intervention, then Xander's four years of quiet solo jumping might be exactly the kind of thing that gets noticed the same way Marie's data got noticed. And I don't know if we want that conversation happening before we understand how the first one's going to resolve."

Buffy turned that over, feeling the weight of it settle somewhere new.

"I think we tell Fury," she said finally. "Quietly, carefully, on our own terms instead of waiting for someone else's satellite to find it first. We just watched what happens when a family gets discovered instead of choosing to disclose. I don't want that for us too."

"That's exactly what I was thinking, and exactly what I was scared to say first." Willow managed something close to a true smile. "You're getting good at this. The big strategic calls."

"I've had practice. Mostly with vampires, but strategy's strategy." Buffy nudged the coffee closer. "Drink that. You're not gonna out-research a cosmic judgment on no sleep."

"I could try."

"You could. I'd still make you drink the coffee first."

Willow finally reached for it, wrapping both hands around the cup like it might actually help, and for a moment the two of them just sat together in the quiet, focused hum of the shop's back room, one more impossible problem folded into a friendship that had, over four years, gotten remarkably practiced at carrying exactly this kind of weight together.

"We should call Xander," Willow said eventually. "Tell him what we're thinking. He deserves to be part of this decision, not just informed of it after."

"Yeah," Buffy agreed, already reaching for her phone. "He does."

***



Chapter 11: Chapter 11



Millie found Ashgrove alone on the porch a little before midnight, the Councilor still awake, still working through a tablet's worth of notes by lamplight while the rest of the house had finally gone quiet.

"You should be resting," Millie said, settling into the chair beside her without waiting for an invitation.

"So should you." Ashgrove didn't look up right away. "I find I do my best thinking after everyone else has stopped needing me to perform confidence for them."

"That's a very specific kind of tired to recognize in somebody else." Millie let the silence sit a moment, the old professional instinct surfacing before she'd consciously decided to use it. "You've been carrying this alone for eight months. Suspecting, not knowing, not able to tell anyone because you couldn't prove it and didn't want to risk being wrong. That's not just diplomatic caution. That's isolation."

Ashgrove's hands stilled on the tablet.

"You're doing the thing," she said, something almost wry under the words. "The reading-people thing. I'd heard you trained in some manner of counsel, back before you settled here permanently."

"Occupational hazard. I don't always manage to turn it off." Millie didn't apologize for it. "You've spent eight months being the only person in Home who knew this was coming, unable to tell my family, unable to fully act, just watching and waiting and hoping you'd guessed right about the timing. That's not a comfortable position for anyone. It's an especially uncomfortable one for somebody who clearly cares more than her job technically requires."

"Is that an accusation?"

"It's an observation. You drove case, alone, into a house full of people you had every reason to expect might be furious with you, because you decided being honest mattered more than being safe." Millie met her eyes. "That's not the profile of somebody who's here to manage us. That's the profile of somebody who's actually trying to protect us, and has been for longer than any of us realized to thank her for it."

Ashgrove was quiet for a long moment, something in her carefully composed bearing finally, briefly, cracking.

"I don't know if it's going to be enough," she admitted, quieter than Millie had heard her yet. "Whatever I argue, whatever precedent Giles finds, whatever your daughter's numbers actually prove — I don't fully control this outcome. I've spent forty years believing I understood the shape of my own authority within Home, and this week I've discovered how much of that understanding was assumption rather than actual knowledge."

"That's a hard thing to sit with."

"It is." Ashgrove set the tablet down finally. "May I ask you something in return? Not as Councilor to family. Just as one tired person to another."

"Go ahead."

"Are you angry with me? Entirely. Not the diplomatic answer. The plain one."

Millie considered the question honestly, the way she considered most things that actually mattered.

"I was, this morning," she said. "I think some part of me still is, a little. Eight months is a long time to have carried a warning I never got to hear. But I've spent this whole day watching you argue our case to people with actual power to hurt us, at unmistakable cost to your own standing in Home, and I don't think anger's actually the useful thing to be feeling anymore. I think gratitude is, even if it's complicated gratitude. You could have stayed quiet indefinitely. You didn't."

"That's more grace than I expected."

"I didn't say it was easy grace. I said it was the useful kind." Millie stood, offering a hand to help Ashgrove up from the chair. "Come inside. Get some actual sleep. Whatever comes tomorrow, you're not facing it as well rested as you'll need to be if you keep sitting out here doing this to yourself all night."

Ashgrove took the offered hand, something like genuine relief crossing her face for the first time since she'd arrived.

"Thank you," she said. "Not just for the invitation. For the reading. I don't believe anyone's actually seen me that clearly in some while."

"Occupational hazard," Millie said again, gentler this time, and led her back inside.

***



Chapter 12: Chapter 12



The monitor came online on the third day, well ahead of Bruce's own estimate, mostly because Tony had shown up with equipment that made "borrowed" a generous description and refused to sleep until the thing actually worked.

"Okay," Bruce said, stepping back from a setup that had taken over most of the workshop's far wall, wires and borrowed satellite hardware feeding into a display that currently showed nothing more dramatic than a flat, steady line. "In theory, this reads the same resonance signature Marie isolated in the archive data, but live. If the Keepers shift from passive observation to something more active — evaluation, judgment, whatever the actual mechanism turns out to be — we should see it here before anyone tells us directly."

"In theory," Tony repeated. "I love a plan that starts with 'in theory.' Really inspires confidence."

"It's a good plan," Marie said, already cross-checking the calibration against her own model. "The baseline's solid. If it spikes, it spikes for a reason."

They didn't have to wait long.

The line jumped at 2:14 in the morning, sharp and sudden, steep enough that Bruce was out of his chair before the alarm he'd rigged even finished its first tone, the whole household stirring awake within minutes at the sound of it.

"That's not routine observation," Marie said, already pulling up comparison data with hands that weren't quite steady. "That's active. That's — Bruce, that's a pattern shift I don't have a baseline for. This isn't like anything in the archive."

"Meaning what," Davy said, already at the door, already positioned between the monitor and the rest of the house without seeming to have decided to do it consciously.

"Meaning something's actually happening," Bruce said, "somewhere in whatever process the Keepers use to evaluate a case like this. I don't know if that's good or bad. I know it's not nothing."

Ashgrove was awake within the minute, pulling her coat on over sleepwear with the brisk efficiency of someone who'd trained herself, long ago, never to be caught unprepared. She studied the readout for a long moment, something crossing her face that Millie recognized, from their conversation the night before, as plain uncertainty rather than practiced calm.

"I don't know what this specifically indicates," she admitted. "I've never had instruments capable of reading it directly before. But if I had to guess, based on everything I do understand about how these evaluations typically proceed—"

"Guess anyway," Fury said. "We don't have time for you to wait until you're certain."

"I believe a decision is being made. Not the final one, necessarily. But something has moved this from theoretical review to active consideration, faster than I expected, faster than is typical." Ashgrove's jaw tightened. "Which means whatever argument we intend to make on this family's behalf, we may have far less time to make it than I originally believed."

"How much less," Millie asked.

"I don't know. Days, possibly. Perhaps less." Ashgrove looked around the room — the whole assembled roster, gods and scientists and kings and librarians, gathered in the middle of the night around a machine reading signals from a decision none of them could see directly. "I would suggest we stop preparing as though we have the luxury of time we don't actually have."

Thor set down the mug he'd somehow already acquired despite the hour, expression settling into something far more serious than his usual easy warmth.

"Then let us stop preparing," he said, "and start deciding what we actually intend to say, together, when the moment to say it finally arrives."

The line on the monitor held steady at its new, higher baseline, an unfamiliar rhythm nobody in the room yet knew how to read, and the household settled, in the small hours of the morning, into the focused, wide-awake work of building an argument for something none of them could see coming, only feel, already, beginning to arrive.

***



Chapter 13: Chapter 13



"You called Jane," Thor said, when Bruce mentioned it almost in passing over breakfast, something in his voice shifting into a careful, hopeful neutrality that fooled nobody at the table.

"I called Jane," Bruce confirmed. "The resonance signature's honestly strange, Thor. Not strange like alien-technology strange, which I could probably muddle through eventually. Strange like it doesn't behave according to any physics I fully understand, and Jane's spent years working on exactly that category of problem. I wasn't going to not call the best astrophysicist I know because it might be awkward."

"I didn't say it would be awkward."

"You said it with your whole face."

Jane arrived that afternoon, travel-worn and visibly curious despite the short notice, and had her laptop open before she'd even finished the introductions, scanning the monitor's readout with the hungry focus of a scientist who'd just been handed a truly new kind of problem.

"Okay, this is incredible," she said, mostly to herself, tracing the spike pattern from the previous night. "This isn't gravitational. It's not electromagnetic, not in any conventional sense. It's — Bruce, this almost reads like the signature's carrying information, not just energy. Structured, not random."

"That's what I thought too. I didn't want to say it out loud until somebody else confirmed I wasn't reaching."

"You're not reaching." Jane looked up, taking in the room properly for the first time — the whiteboard, the assembled roster, the particular tension of a house mid-crisis. "Somebody want to catch me up properly? Thor's version on the flight over was mostly 'a family did something remarkable and now powerful people are deciding whether to reward or punish them for it,' which, no offense, Thor, is not a briefing."

"It was an accurate summary."

"It was three sentences."

"Accurate three sentences."

Marie walked her through the actual data while Jane listened with the particular stillness of someone filing away every detail for later use, occasionally interrupting with a question sharp enough that Marie had to actually stop and think before answering.

"This is honestly one of the best-documented first-contact-adjacent signal sets I've ever seen," Jane said finally. "I'd kill for a chance to actually study this properly, under better circumstances. Under these circumstances, I'd settle for helping you people not lose everything you've built before you get the chance to have this fight on fair terms."

*

T'Challa's call to Wakanda that evening was brief, and the response arrived far faster than protocol usually allowed, which told Charlie something about how seriously this was already being taken before M'Baku ever set foot through the gate.

He arrived imposing in a way that had nothing to do with size, though he had that too, taking in the assembled roster with an expression that gave away exactly nothing until he'd finished his own careful assessment of the room.

"So," he said, finally, to T'Challa, in a tone that carried the worn-in weight of an old argument neither of them needed to fully restate. "Wakanda involves itself in another outside power's business."

"Wakanda involves itself in a family's business, at their invitation, because the alternative to us being here is them facing this judgment with no advocate who's ever successfully argued this exact case before."

"You mean our case. Vibranium, and every empire that ever decided they were owed it." M'Baku's gaze swept the room, landing eventually on Millie. His voice dropped, sudden and grave. "Cross my king on this, and I will personally see to it that—" He let the silence hang a beat too long, watching Millie's face carefully. "—that he never lets me hear the end of it, because he will insist I stop threatening his allies within the first five minutes of meeting them. I'm kidding. Mostly. I don't say this to diminish your situation. I say it because I want you to understand exactly what kind of ally you have in my king. He argues from experience, not sentiment. That should reassure you more than it worries you."

"Noted," Millie said, carefully.

"Good." M'Baku dropped into an empty chair with the unhurried confidence of a man entirely comfortable being the most skeptical person in any room. "Now. Tell me everything, and don't soften it for my benefit. If I'm advising my king on whether this fight's worth Wakanda's name attached to it, I need the actual shape of the danger, not the version built to make me feel generous."

Watching from across the room, Fury cracked something close to a plain smile — the first genuine one all week.

"I like him," he said, to nobody in particular.

"Most people eventually do," T'Challa said. "It generally takes a good deal longer than an afternoon, however."

"I contain multitudes," M'Baku said, entirely unbothered, already reaching for the nearest data printout with the same hungry seriousness Jane had brought to the monitor readout an hour earlier, and the room, already crowded with gods and scientists and kings, made room for two more voices without much trouble at all.

***



Chapter 14: Chapter 14



Shuri came through two days later, unable to stay away once M'Baku's reports back to Wakanda made clear exactly what kind of signal analysis problem had presented itself, and she took one look at Jane's data before either of them had properly finished introductions.

"You said structured," she said, already pulling up her own equipment, far more compact and far more advanced than anything currently occupying Bruce's workshop wall. "Structured like language, or structured like a heartbeat? Because those are very different problems, and I would like to know which one I am solving before I get too excited about solving it."

"Honestly not sure yet," Jane admitted. "There's a rhythm to it, but I can't tell if the rhythm's the message or just the carrier wave for something underneath."

"Then let us find out." Shuri had her equipment interfaced with Bruce's monitor within twenty minutes, working alongside Jane and Marie with the easy, rapid-fire shorthand of three brilliant people who'd each independently decided the fastest way through a problem was simply to stop being polite about interrupting each other's sentences.

"Okay," Shuri said, an hour in, leaning back from the display with an expression that mixed honest excitement and plain unease in roughly equal measure. "It's not a heartbeat. It's closer to a heartbeat that's trying to become a sentence. I don't think whoever's generating this signal has fully decided what they want to say yet. I think we're watching the actual formation of a decision, not just its aftermath."

The room went quiet at that.

"Can we read it," Millie asked. "Once it does decide."

"Perhaps. I do not know your Keepers' language, obviously. But structure this deliberate does not stay unreadable forever, not to people determined enough to keep working at it." Shuri glanced at Jane, something like unmistakable respect passing between them. "I would like to keep working at it. If that is acceptable."

"More than acceptable," Bruce said. "Honestly a relief. I was starting to feel out of my depth."

"You were doing excellent work regardless," Shuri said, generous about it in a way that clearly cost her nothing, a grin already tugging at one corner of her mouth. "I just have better toys."

*

Wanda arrived the following morning, summoned by a call from Steve that had been far more urgent than his usual calm phrasing, Vision a quiet, steady step behind her, and stood in front of the monitor's readout for a long moment before saying anything at all, eyes distant in the particular way that meant she was reading something the instruments couldn't quite capture.

"It's not just data," she said finally. "There's feeling under this. Genuine feeling, not just structure. Whatever's forming this signal — I think it's actually uncertain. Really torn. That's not something a machine generates. That's something alive, wrestling with an actual decision."

"Can you tell which way it's leaning," Ashgrove asked, careful, hopeful despite herself.

Wanda was quiet a long moment, concentration deepening.

"No," she said finally, something almost apologetic in it. "It's too far away, and too foreign, for me to read that clearly. But I can tell you it's not cold. Whatever's deciding this isn't deciding it coldly. That has to count for something."

"It counts for quite a lot, actually," Thor said, quiet, something in his voice carrying old, dearly bought knowledge. "I have stood before powers who judged coldly. This is not that. Cold judgment does not agonize over its own decision."

Vision, who had been quietly studying the readout himself, offered his own careful observation. "I don't have enough to say what this is. But I'd caution against reading too much into the uncertainty itself, one way or the other. My own mind began as something far less settled than what I eventually became. Uncertainty, on its own, tells you very little except that something is still becoming. It doesn't yet tell you what."

"Meaning what, exactly," Millie asked.

"Meaning I don't want to hand you false comfort dressed up as analysis," Vision said. "It would be easy to say the uncertainty means good news is still possible. It would be equally easy to say it means nothing at all. Honestly, I don't know which. I only know that treating uncertainty itself as evidence, in either direction, is a mistake I've watched people make before, and I'd rather not make it here."

Wanda glanced at him, something warm passing between them regardless, the old ease of two minds that had learned, over time, to trust each other's particular way of seeing things — including, especially, each other's honesty about the limits of what either of them actually knew.

Sam, arriving in time to catch the tail end of it, leaned against the doorframe with the easy, grounded skepticism of a man who'd learned to stay useful in rooms a good deal more chaotic than this one.

"So the good news is, whatever's judging you actually cares," he said. "The bad news is, caring and deciding in your favor aren't the same thing, and I've watched plenty of people agonize their way into a decision I really wished they hadn't made."

"Comforting," Davy muttered.

"I'm an honest-comfort guy, not a false-comfort guy. Figured you'd earned honest by now, given the week you've had." Sam pushed off the doorframe, crossing to the coffee pot with the unhurried confidence of someone making himself useful in the most practical way available. "For what it's worth, though — most people, cold or not, respond better to a room full of folks who clearly love each other than to one that's falling apart under pressure. You're doing better than you probably feel like you're doing."

The room gave way to that, small and steadying, one more voice added to a household that had, by now, gotten remarkably good at making space for exactly the kind of help each new arrival actually brought with them.

***



Chapter 15: Chapter 15



Shuri found the flaw in their whole working theory four days later, and it took her a full ten minutes of staring at her own results before she trusted them enough to say anything out loud.

"I do not think this is a judgment," she said finally, into a workshop gone quiet around her. "I have been modeling this as an evaluative process — variables being weighed, a decision converging toward a verdict. It does not converge that way. It doesn't move like reasoning. It moves like growth."

Jane was beside her within seconds, pulling up the same dataset, running her own comparison. "She's right. Look at the structure density over time — it's not resolving toward a conclusion, it's *accumulating*. That's not how decisions build. That's how—"

"That's how something develops," Wanda finished, quiet, something in her voice gone very still. "I told you it felt alive. I didn't mean alive like a mind making a choice. I think I meant alive like something actually becoming."

Vision, standing near the door, said nothing for a moment, something almost rueful in his expression. "I told this room, four days ago, that uncertainty alone couldn't tell us what we were looking at. I didn't expect to be quite this right about it."

"You weren't wrong to be cautious," Wanda said, glancing at him. "You just didn't have the rest of the data yet. None of us did."

The room absorbed the larger shape of it slowly, the truth too large to take in all at once.
