There was a pause, longer than usual, the particular quiet of someone processing something that had entirely, completely surprised them.

*what?* Dawn said, finally, warmth and astonishment tangled together in the single word. *i have a brother?*

Wanda couldn't quite help the smile that broke across her face. "Maybe. It's not confirmed. Giles found an old report, decades old, describing something that sounds a lot like the Keepers dealing with a previous child. We don't know for certain it's the same beings."

*but it might be.* Dawn's light pulsed, quick and searching, something new and delighted moving through it. *i did not expect that. i had wondered where i came from. i had not wondered if i came from somewhere with someone else in it too.*

"How does it feel? Finding that out?"

*strange. good-strange, i think. i have had this whole family, freely given, since my very first night. i had not imagined there might also be a family i was simply born into, the way you all were born into yours.* A pause, thoughtful. *do you think he would like me? if it is true?*

"I don't know," Wanda admitted, honest as always. "I don't know anything about him beyond a decades-old report describing some pretty significant mischief and a fairly dramatic parental intervention. But for what it's worth — if he's anything like you at all, I'd guess yes."

*i would like to meet him. eventually. carefully, the way i met the keepers.* Dawn's light warmed further, something bright and hopeful spreading through it. *i did not know, until just now, that i might have been missing something. i find i am glad to have something new to hope for.*

Millie, listening from a few steps away, felt her own chest tighten with something easy and complicated — pride, and protectiveness, and the same, novel vertigo of realizing her family had just, potentially, gotten larger in a direction none of them could have predicted.

"Whatever you find," she said, stepping closer, "however this turns out — you know this family doesn't shrink to make room for another one. It just gets bigger."

*i know,* Dawn said, certain and warm. *i think that might be my favorite thing about existing, so far. that there always seems to be room for more.*

Xander hadn't said much, but he spoke now, something wry and affectionate in his voice. "For the record, if this kid turns out to be even half as dramatic as that old report makes him sound, I feel like we should probably prepare accordingly. Family reunions with reformed near-omnipotent troublemakers seem like exactly the kind of thing that goes sideways if nobody plans ahead."

"Noted," Fury said, dry. "Adding it to the list."

"Also," Faith added, deadpan, "gonna need somebody to clarify what 'juvenile' means on a scale where the guy can apparently rearrange whole planets for fun. Because if we're doing a family reunion, I want to know up front whether I'm buying a onesie or building a bunker."

"Both," Tony said, without missing a beat. "Get both. I'll expense it."

*i do not think i require a onesie,* Dawn put in, warm amusement threading through the words, clearly delighted to have found something to tease them back about. *though i confess i am curious what one is.*

"Don't ask Tony to explain it," Willow said. "You'll regret it."

"I would give an excellent explanation," Tony said, wounded.

"You would give a forty-minute explanation involving at least three unnecessary diagrams."

"That's not a genuine rebuttal, that's just a description of my strengths."

The household folded into easy, hopeful speculation after that, Dawn's quiet wonder at the thought of a brother spreading warm through the whole gathered evening, one more strange and delightful possibility folded into a year that had, by now, made a habit of exceeding everyone's expectations.

***



Chapter 66: Chapter 66



Giles's research continued through the following weeks, the Starfleet fragment proving frustratingly incomplete despite his no small amount of persistence, and he called again on a Tuesday evening with the particular energy of a man who'd found something worth interrupting dinner for.

"I've located a second reference," he said, without preamble. "Far more recent — a follow-up note, filed decades after the original incident, suggesting the boy in question was eventually permitted limited contact with the wider universe again, under careful supervision. The note describes him as 'a good deal matured, though prone still to theatrical excess.'"

"So he's still around," Xander said. "Somewhere. Not locked away forever."

"It would appear so, yes. Though I have no location, no name beyond what the original report used, and no indication of how one might actually go about locating him, should Dawn's hope to meet him ever move from speculation to true plan."

Willow was already working through the implications with her usual rapid focus. "If he's had limited contact with the wider universe, that means Starfleet's had some ongoing relationship with whatever species the Keepers actually are. That's a whole diplomatic channel that might already exist, if we can figure out how to access it properly."

"A rather large 'if,'" Giles cautioned. "I don't relish the idea of contacting an organization from an entirely different fictional — I do apologize, entirely different *actual* framework of reality, given how thoroughly this year has demonstrated my old assumptions about fiction versus fact were a great deal too narrow."

"Nobody relishes it," Fury said, joining the call from the homestead's kitchen. "But if there's an honest channel, plain precedent for contact, that's worth pursuing carefully. I've got contacts who might be able to at least confirm whether Starfleet, or whatever equivalent exists in whatever reality that report came from, is even reachable from here."

"Carefully," Millie emphasized. "This isn't a thread to pull without unmistakable thought behind it. Dawn's hope for a brother is genuine. I don't want that hope handled carelessly, chasing a lead that might lead nowhere, or worse, somewhere we're not prepared for."

The household eased into careful, calm planning once more, the familiar shape of it now — not rushing, not forcing an outcome, simply continuing the steady, hopeful work of following a true thread wherever it honestly led, one careful step at a time.

***



Chapter 67: Chapter 67



Cent's own life continued alongside Dawn's story, the two threads tangled together now in ways she'd stopped trying to fully separate, and she caught herself, one autumn afternoon, back at her orbital calculations for the first time in months, Marie beside her at the same workshop whiteboard that had once tracked murder theories and now tracked, instead, satellite recovery windows for a job that had been waiting without hurry for her attention.

"Feels good," she admitted, chalk in hand, working through a burn calculation with true pleasure. "Getting back to this. I love Dawn. I love all of it. But I missed just being an engineer for a while too."

"You're allowed both," Marie said, watching her work with open affection. "Nobody's asking you to choose."

"I know. Still feels like relearning something, some days. How to hold the astonishing and the ordinary at the same time without either one crowding the other out."

"You're doing it, though. Look at you. Full orbital recovery calculation, and you haven't once stopped mid-equation to check the field monitor today."

"I checked it twice, actually."

"Twice is restraint, for you, these days."

Cent laughed, honest and warm, and set down the chalk long enough to stretch properly, the workshop's familiar quiet settling around them the way it always had, incredible circumstances layered now, comfortably, over the same steady friendship that had carried them both through much harder stretches than this one.

Joe stuck his head in partway through the afternoon, two coffees balanced carefully in one hand. "Figured you two forgot to eat again. Confirmed by the state of this room, for the record — not a single plate in sight."

"We were being productive," Cent said, accepting the coffee gratefully, tipping her face up for a quick kiss before he could set the second cup down for Marie. "Productivity waits for no sandwich."

"Productivity can wait exactly as long as it takes to eat something. I brought those too. They're in my other hand, which you'd have noticed if you weren't both staring at a whiteboard like it owes you money."

Marie grinned, taking her own coffee with honest gratitude. "You're the only reason either of us remembers meals exist some days. I keep telling Cent she should marry you just for the coffee delivery service alone."

"Noted," Joe said, dry, though something warm passed between him and Cent regardless. "I'll add it to my list of selling points."

They worked through the rest of the afternoon together, orbital math and easy company tangled comfortably into the same quiet hours, Dawn's warm light visible through the workshop window, steady and steady above the distant field, one more ordinary, striking day folded into a life that had, against every reasonable expectation, found room for everything that mattered.

***



Chapter 68: Chapter 68



Fury's contacts came back with something two weeks later, significantly stranger than anyone had prepared for — not a direct channel to Starfleet itself, but a name, passed along through layers of intermediaries so careful and indirect that Fury admitted, reporting it, he had no plain confidence in the chain of custody.

"Someone called the Corbomite Maneuver contact," he said, dry despite the strangeness of the phrase. "Old code name, apparently, from whatever agency first handled the original incident's fallout decades back. Whoever's behind it claims limited ability to relay a message, no promises about response time, no guarantee it even reaches the right hands."

"It's something, though," Willow said. "An unmistakable thread, not just theory."

"It's something," Fury agreed. "I'd rather manage expectations carefully than promise more than this actually is."

Dawn, listening through Wanda's relay, considered the news with her usual thoughtful care.

*i would like to send something,* she said, finally. *even if it does not reach him. even if it takes a good deal of time. i think i would regret not trying, more than i would regret trying and hearing nothing back.*

"What would you want to say," Millie asked, gentle.

Dawn was quiet a long moment, composing something with obvious care.

*hello,* she said, finally. *i am told you may be my brother. i do not know if that is true, or what it means if it is. i only know that i am glad to exist, and that i have found a family here who taught me that gladness is worth sharing, freely, with anyone willing to receive it. i would like to know you, if you are willing. i am in no hurry. i have already learned that the best things are usually worth waiting for.*

The message went out through Fury's careful, uncertain channel two days later, and the household settled back into unhurried waiting once more — not anxious this time, Charlie noticed, watching the family's easy rhythm continue undisturbed by the uncertainty. Just hopeful, steady, willing to hold the question open for as long as it took.

"Feels like the right message," Millie said, that evening, watching Dawn's light pulse calm and content above the field. "Whatever comes of it."

"Yeah," Davy agreed. "Feels like exactly who she is, put into words. Can't ask for better than that, whatever happens next."

***



Chapter 69: Chapter 69



Winter arrived at the homestead with its own quiet rhythm, the household settling into a season far calmer than the remarkable year that had preceded it — Dawn's message sent and, for now, unanswered, the Keepers' verdict still pending, the whole family learning, gradually, how to hold significant uncertainty as simply one more ordinary thread in an otherwise full and settled life.

Charlie found his father in the barn one snow-dusted morning, running through the Cessna's winterizing checklist with the same unhurried thoroughness he brought to everything.

"Feels different this year," Charlie said, helping drain the last of the fuel lines. "Winter, I mean. Used to feel like just waiting out the cold. Feels like something fuller now."

"Fuller how?"

"Don't know exactly. Just — more in it, I guess. More family, more hope, more things worth being even-tempered for." Charlie considered the thought properly as he said it. "Feels like the waiting itself changed shape, somewhere along the way. Used to feel like holding my breath. Now it just feels like living, while something good takes its own time arriving."

His father considered that, something quietly proud in his expression. "That's a good way to put it. Wish I'd had those words for it myself, some years back, waiting on things a good deal less certain to turn out well."

"You mean Uncle Max."

"Among other things." His father set down the fuel cap, met Charlie's eyes properly. "I think he'd have liked this winter. All of it. The waiting that doesn't feel like holding your breath anymore."

Charlie felt something warm settle in his chest at that, an old grief finally, fully at peace rather than merely managed.

"Yeah," he said. "I think he would have too."

They finished the winterizing together in comfortable quiet, snow beginning to fall soft and steady outside the barn doors, and Charlie thought, walking back toward the house afterward, that whatever the coming months held — an answer from a possible brother, a verdict from the Keepers, whatever else this astonishing, ordinary life still had waiting — he'd learned, this year, exactly how to hold it all without fear crowding out the wonder.

***



Chapter 70: Chapter 70



The response arrived in early spring, nearly four months after Dawn's message went out, and it came not through Fury's careful channel but directly, the same way the Keepers themselves had first arrived — pressure shifting slowly in the open air above the field, gathering into something the household had, by now, learned to recognize immediately.

But this presence felt different from the Keepers' vast, ancient weight — younger, brighter, carrying an energy that read, even before it fully arrived, as something closer to eager than solemn.

*hello,* a new voice said, arriving in every mind present with a great deal more flourish than the Keepers had ever employed, something theatrical and delighted threading through the greeting. *i am told i have a sister. i confess i did not expect that news, in all my substantial years of expecting rather a lot of things. but here i am. i do hope i am not terribly late. i understand punctuality is valued, among your kind, though i've never quite mastered the habit myself.*

Dawn's light flared, bright and startled and unmistakably delighted. *you came.*

*of course i came. one does not simply ignore the discovery of a sibling, particularly not one so recently and so remarkably emerged. i have heard rather a lot about you, actually, in the time since your message reached me — genuine inquiry on my part, i confess, once i understood what i was dealing with.* A pause, something warmer beneath the theatrical energy. *i am glad you exist. i did not expect to feel that so plainly, so quickly. but there it is.*

Millie watched the exchange with something caught between wonder and careful maternal wariness, old instinct assessing the new presence even as her heart, plainly, was already softening toward it.

"You're welcome here," she said, stepping forward. "Whoever you are, whatever you've been through. Dawn's hoped for this for months now."

*how marvelous. a whole assembled welcoming committee, and not a single one of you looking especially terrified, which i confess is something of a novel experience for me.* The presence seemed to consider the gathered crowd properly for the first time, something delighted and faintly wicked threading through the observation. *gods, kings, a scientist who appears to be quietly assessing whether i can be contained by conventional engineering — you needn't bother, dear, i checked several centuries ago and the answer remains no — and an entire field positively humming with the particular energy of people who've already survived one improbable cosmic ordeal this year and are bracing, i suspect, for a second.*

Fury, arms crossed, studied the presence with the particular skepticism of a man who'd read every scrap of the fragmentary Starfleet report twice. "Before this goes any further — I want it understood, plainly, that we don't take kindly to being toyed with. Whatever games you used to play."

Something shifted in the presence, fast and sharp, an old reflex surfacing before it could be caught — *do not presume to instruct me on what i will or will not —* — and then, just as quickly, it stopped itself, the sentence left hanging, unfinished, the theatrical brightness dimming into something considerably more careful.

*forgive me. old habit. i am told my temper used to arrive rather faster than my judgment did, and apparently it has not entirely forgotten the shortcut.* A pause, genuinely rueful this time. *i am not that anymore. i am trying very hard not to be. but i thought it more honest to let you see the old reflex once, rather than pretend it never existed at all.*

"Are we bracing correctly," Tony asked, dry, though something in his posture had gone slightly more alert.

*only slightly. i've been assured, at no small amount of length and by parties whose judgment i've learned to trust, that theatrics are not the same thing as trouble, and that i am rather better at telling the two apart these days than i once was. i intend to prove it. mostly by being delightful instead of destructive, which i'm reliably informed is the preferred arrangement.*

"That's reassuring," Fury said, still visibly unconvinced.

*it should be. i've had rather a long time to practice the distinction.* The theatrical brightness softened, just slightly, something more sincere surfacing beneath it. *and you are the family who taught her that gladness is worth sharing freely,* the presence added, warmth threading now through what had been pure performance a moment before. *i have been rather a long time learning that lesson myself, through much harder means than she seems to have needed. i find i am glad she had gentler teachers than i did. i do intend to remain entertaining regardless. reform, in my experience, need not mean becoming dull.*

"You can learn it here too," Wanda offered. "If you want to stay a while. Get to know her properly."

There was a pause, and when the presence spoke again, something in its tone had shifted into true, unguarded hope, the theatrical flourish still very much present underneath it.

*i believe,* it said, slowly, delight and sincerity tangled together in equal, unmistakable measure, *i would like that very much indeed. do warn me, though, if i overstay my welcome. i'm told i have historically required the warning rather bluntly stated.*

***



Chapter 71: Chapter 71



Trelane — he offered the name within his first hour at the homestead, delivered with a small, theatrical bow that needed no physical body to somehow still land as a bow — took to the household with the enthusiasm of someone who'd apparently been waiting centuries for exactly this kind of chaos-free chaos to fall into.

He insisted, first, on proper introductions — every single person in the room, by name, by role, in an order he seemed to determine by some private logic of his own, tutting audibly when Charlie tried to shortcut the process by simply gesturing at a cluster of people at once. "Tut, tut. That will not do at all. I have been quite derelict in my social observances already, arriving without so much as a calling card. I will not compound the offense by lumping you all together like so much luggage."

"You're staying, then," Charlie said, once the introductions had finally, exhaustively concluded, still adjusting to the idea of a near-omnipotent brother-in-law-adjacent presence becoming a regular fixture.

*If you'll have me. I confess I've grown rather fond of this little planet over the centuries, in my own occasionally destructive way. I'd like the chance to be fond of it properly this time. Constructively, even, should the mood strike.*

"You say that like it's a foreign concept," Faith said, arms crossed, still visibly reserving judgment.

*It rather was, for longer than I'd like to admit. But I've had a good deal of time to reconsider my hobbies.* Something warmer threaded through the theatrical flourish. *And now I have a sister. That does rather reorganize one's priorities, I've found. Suddenly there's someone whose opinion of me actually matters.*

Dawn's light pulsed warm beside him — beside the gathered, shimmering presence that had, over the following days, learned to settle into something more consistently visible, a shape that suggested a person without ever quite committing to one, features shifting playfully depending on his mood.

*he tells excellent stories,* Dawn reported, delighted, to Millie that evening. *about planets. about being terribly bored for very long stretches of time. about the exact consequences of underestimating small, determined starship crews.*

"Are the stories true," Millie asked, careful.

*Mostly,* Trelane said, entirely unbothered by the qualifier. *I may have improved one or two details in the retelling. A storyteller's prerogative, surely.*

"He also cheats at cards," Tony reported, arriving from the porch with the precise aggrieved energy of a man who'd just lost several consecutive hands. "Cannot prove it. Absolutely certain of it."

*I would never cheat. I simply understand probability significantly better than any of you, having had rather more time to study it.*

"That's cheating with extra steps."

*That's expertise, Anthony. There's a distinction, and I'll thank you to learn it before our next game.*

The bickering sank into easy, established rhythm within days, Trelane folding into the household's chaos with the same, delighted ease of someone who'd finally found people willing to be exasperated with him rather than afraid of him — and Dawn, watching her brother charm and needle and plainly, warmly connect with everyone he met, realized she was, more and more, simply glad, in the same uncomplicated way she'd been glad since her very first hello, that her family had, once again, gotten a little bigger.

***



Chapter 72: Chapter 72



Giles finally made the trip out to the homestead properly two weeks after Trelane's arrival, having spent the intervening fortnight fielding an increasingly bizarre stream of updates over the phone and deciding, at last, that some things completely required in-person assessment.

He found Trelane mid-story, holding court on the porch for an audience that included Charlie, Cent, and a visibly skeptical Faith, gesturing with theatrical flourish through some tale of centuries-old mischief that had, by the sound of it, once involved an entire star system's worth of confused sailors.

Giles stopped in the doorway, took in the scene — the shimmering, shifting presence, the delighted audience, Dawn's warm light pulsing fondly nearby — and allowed himself a long, weary sigh before speaking.

"By the pricking of my thumbs," he said, dry, mostly to himself, "something wicked this way comes."

Trelane's attention snapped toward him immediately, delighted. *Oh, I do like him already. A man who quotes the Scottish play unprompted. How refreshing.*

"I am aware of what I said," Giles replied, unmoved, though something suspiciously close to amusement tugged at the corner of his mouth. "I chose it deliberately. I've read your file, such as it is. I'm rather relieved to find the description undersold you slightly, on the charm front, if nothing else."

*High praise, from a man who clearly reads for pleasure rather than obligation. I approve of you already.*

"I haven't decided yet whether that's mutual," Giles said, settling into the nearest chair, though his tone had softened far from its opening dryness. "I understand you're the reason for a rather significant addition to the Watchers' restricted archive, once I finish properly documenting this. I don't suppose you'd be willing to fill in the substantial gaps in that decades-old Starfleet report."

*I would be delighted. Properly delighted, mind, not the theatrical variety — I so rarely get an audience who actually wants the accurate version of events rather than the exciting one.* Trelane's shifting form settled, briefly, into something almost solemn. *Though I warn you, some of it reflects rather poorly on me. I've made my peace with that. I'm not certain you will, quite so easily.*

"I've spent thirty years reading about a good deal worse redemption arcs than yours appears to be," Giles said. "I believe I'll manage."

Faith, watching the exchange with open amusement, leaned over toward Charlie. "Feels like we just got a second Watcher on staff, purely for cosmic sibling management."

"Feels like exactly that, yeah," Charlie agreed, grinning, and the porch gave way to easy, warm conversation, Giles's careful questions and Trelane's truly earnest answers weaving together into something that felt, by the evening's end, remarkably like the beginning of an honest friendship.

***



Chapter 73: Chapter 73



The Giles-Trelane correspondence became its own small, delightful fixture of the household over the following weeks, phone calls and in-person visits alike settling into a rhythm built substantially on literary sparring that left most of the rest of the family cheerfully lost.

"He quoted Prospero at me yesterday," Giles reported, to Xander, with the particular weary satisfaction of a man who'd found an opponent worthy of his effort. "Entirely unprompted, in the middle of describing an incident involving a rather unfortunate encounter with a Klingon vessel. 'We are such stuff as dreams are made on,' delivered with a straight face, immediately followed by a joke about torpedo trajectories."

"Did you have a comeback?"

"Naturally. One does not survive a Watcher's education without a reasonably deep well of Shakespeare to draw from." Giles allowed himself a small, satisfied smile. "I responded with Puck. 'Lord, what fools these mortals be.' He laughed for a full minute. I believe I've found the one being in this household capable of properly appreciating my particular sense of humor."

Trelane, arriving mid-conversation with his usual theatrical flourish, caught the tail end of it with evident delight. *He speaks of me fondly. I'm touched. Truly. Do go on.*

"I was merely noting your fondness for quoting a man who's been dead for centuries."

*A man who understood theater a great deal better than most of your current century manages, if I'm honest. We have quite a lot in common, he and I. Both of us rather partial to a well-timed dramatic reveal.*

"You're doing it right now," Faith observed, from across the room.

*Naturally. Consistency is a virtue.*

Dawn, watching the exchange with obvious warmth, leaned toward Wanda. "I think this might be my favorite thing he's found here. Not just family. An actual proper sparring partner."

"Everyone deserves one of those," Wanda agreed, smiling. "Glad he found his in Giles, of all people."

"Honestly," Xander said, "kind of glad too. Giles needed somebody who could keep up with him. Rest of us mostly just nod along and hope we're following."

The banter rolled on through the evening, Shakespeare and starship anecdotes tangling together into something that had become, remarkably quickly, one of the household's most reliable sources of easy delight — one more small, unexpected gift folded into a year that had, by now, made something of a habit of them.

***



Chapter 74: Chapter 74



Trelane's presence brought its own complications along with its delights, and the first true one arrived roughly a month into his stay, in the form of a question Natasha raised carefully at one of the household's regular planning sessions.

"He's powerful," she said, without preamble. "Honestly, significantly powerful, in ways we still don't fully understand. I don't say that as an accusation. I say it because we spent months building careful security around Dawn's presence, and I don't think we've had an equivalent conversation about his."

"You think he's a risk," Millie said, not quite a question.

"I think he's an unknown, and unknowns deserve careful thought regardless of how charming they are at dinner." Natasha's tone stayed level, professional. "I like him. Really. That doesn't mean I've stopped doing my job."

Something flickered across Trelane's form at that, quick and cold — *and who, precisely, gave you leave to call me an unknown, as though i were some —* — before it caught itself, the sentence swallowed whole, the flicker gone almost before Charlie was certain he'd seen it at all.

*forgive that.* His voice, when it returned, had lost its usual brightness entirely, something genuinely shaken underneath the apology. *old habits are hard to break, it seems, even months in. i had rather hoped that particular one had finally died out. apparently it simply sleeps lighter than i'd like.*

"You okay?" Faith asked, careful, watching him with real concern rather than her usual wary assessment.

*i will be. i find i dislike it intensely, being reminded how close the old shape still sits beneath this one. but i would rather you see it happen, honestly, than pretend it does not still happen at all.*

Trelane, who'd been listening from the doorway with more gravity than his usual theatrical bearing suggested, stepped forward before anyone else could respond.

*You're entirely right to raise it,* he said, something serious and unhurried replacing his usual flourish. *I would think less of you if you hadn't. I spent no small number of centuries being exactly the unknown you're worried about, and I don't expect trust simply because I've decided, rather belatedly, to deserve it.*

"What would help," Fury asked, direct. "Concretely. What would actually let us verify the trust instead of just hoping it holds."

*I would suggest asking my parents,* Trelane said, something wry beneath the seriousness. *They've had rather more practice than anyone alive at determining whether my reform is honest or merely convincing. I don't say that flippantly. I say it because I understand, better than most, how thin the line can look from the outside, and I'd rather you verify it properly than simply extend faith I haven't fully earned yet.*

Ashgrove, listening with unmistakable interest, offered her own careful addition. "I could formally request the Keepers' assessment, the next time contact occurs. Given their evident investment in how this unfolds, I suspect they'd be willing to offer genuine reassurance, one way or the other."

"I'd want that," Natasha said. "Not out of distrust specifically. Out of due diligence, the same diligence we'd extend to anyone this powerful, family or not."

*Due diligence,* Trelane repeated, something almost fond in his voice despite the scrutiny. *What a refreshingly unglamorous phrase. I find I rather like it. Rather like you, for insisting on it instead of simply accepting my no small amount of charm at face value.*

"Charm's not evidence," Natasha said, though something in her tone had warmed slightly.

*No,* Trelane agreed. *But it's a start, isn't it. Charm now, verification to follow. I believe that's rather how trust is meant to work, in the end.*

The household folded into that arrangement with true, careful thoughtfulness — not suspicion exactly, but the exact, mature caution of people who'd learned, this past year, that sincere wonder and honest diligence weren't opposites, but partners, each one making the other more trustworthy for having been held together.

***



Chapter 75: Chapter 75



Buffy's visits continued through the spring, regular now, comfortable in a way that still occasionally caught her off guard given how little either she or Dawn understood the actual shape of their connection.

"I brought homework this time," she announced, settling into the grass beside the light with the easy familiarity of a dozen previous visits. "Figured if I'm gonna keep coming out here, I should probably stop falling behind on precalc every time I do."

*i could help,* Dawn offered, warmth threading through the words. *i find i am rather good with numbers, now that i have had proper time to understand them.*

"You want to help me with precalc."

*i would like to try. i have very little else to occupy quiet afternoons, and i confess i've grown curious about the precise things that occupy yours.*

They worked through the problem set together, Dawn's calm, careful explanations proving much more effective than Buffy's textbook had managed all semester, and by the time they finished, Buffy noticed she was laughing, entirely delighted, at the strange, wonderful shape of her life these days — cosmic entity tutoring, sandwiched between vampire slaying and actual high school.

"You'd have made a good teacher," she said, packing up her things. "If that's a thing you could be, I mean. Not sure how that works, for you."

*i don't know yet either. i find i like the idea, though. helping people understand things properly, calmly, the way i was helped to understand myself.* A thoughtful pause. *perhaps that is simply what i am, in part. someone who likes to help things make sense.*

"That tracks," Buffy said, smiling. "Definitely tracks, based on everything I've seen."

She lingered a while longer before heading back, the easy, unhurried quiet that had become simply how these visits went, and caught herself, walking back toward the house, turning over the same unanswered question that had followed her since their very first meeting — what exactly this connection was, why it mattered so plainly and so immediately, why some quiet, certain part of her had known, from the very first moment the light reached toward her, that this particular relationship was one she'd be tending for a very long time to come.

She didn't have an answer. She'd stopped needing one, mostly, content to simply let the connection be exactly what it already, clearly was — plain, warm, worth returning for, regardless of whether either of them ever fully understood why.

***



Chapter 76: Chapter 76



The Keepers returned in early summer, significantly sooner than their own vague timeline had suggested, and this time their arrival carried a different quality — less vast and unknowable, more measured, the same weight of beings who'd actually reached a decision rather than simply gathering more information.

*we have deliberated,* the presence said, once the household had gathered properly in the field, Dawn and Trelane both present, the whole incredible family standing together as they'd learned, by now, to stand for everything that mattered. *regarding the child's nature, and regarding the child who arrived to claim kinship with her.*

Ashgrove stepped forward, formal despite months of easy familiarity with the household around her. "We're ready to hear it."

*the emergence remains unprecedented. we do not fully understand its cause, nor can we promise it will not happen again, elsewhere, under similar conditions. this uncertainty no longer troubles us the way it once did. we have observed this family's stewardship of it for the better part of a year, and we find ourselves satisfied that uncertainty, properly tended, need not become danger.*

Millie felt Davy's hand tighten in hers, old wariness finally, fully easing.

*regarding the elder child—* the presence continued, and something in Trelane's shimmering form went very still, centuries of practiced theatrical confidence briefly, entirely absent. *we have watched his conduct here as closely as we watched hers. we find unmistakable, sustained change, not merely convincing performance. this assessment was not made lightly, nor swiftly. we required a good deal of evidence before trusting it.*

*thank you,* Trelane said, quiet, none of his usual flourish present in the two simple words.

*you need not thank us for observing truth accurately. you have earned this yourself, through substantial effort, over real time.* A pause, something almost gentle in the vast, ancient consideration. *we are... glad, to find both our children finding their way toward true goodness, however different the paths that brought them there.*

The word landed heavy in the gathered quiet — *our children* — an acknowledgment of family Charlie understood, watching Dawn's light flare bright with emotion beside her brother's briefly solid, briefly human-shaped form, mattered more than any formal verdict ever could have.

"What happens now," Millie asked, gentle.

*now, we continue watching, as we always have. but we do so with far more hope than uncertainty, for the first time in longer than i believe either of my children would find comfortable being told.* Something that might, in a being so vast, have been honest warmth threaded through the final words. *live well. both of you. we will return, in time, simply to see how well you're managing it.*

The presence withdrew slowly, the field settling back into ordinary summer quiet, and the household gathered close around Dawn and Trelane both, the whole striking family finally, fully at peace with a verdict that had taken the better part of a year to arrive, and had been, in the end, exactly as hopeful as the family itself had never quite stopped believing it could be.

***



Chapter 77: Chapter 77



Summer eased into the homestead with the particular ease of a household no longer bracing for the next crisis, and Charlie realized he was, more days than not, simply living inside the ordinary shape of it — chores, meals, the steady accumulation of small, unremarkable pleasures that had once felt impossible to imagine amid everything else.

Clara had moved out to the homestead properly that June, the two of them taking over the smaller guest house near the barn with an ease that surprised neither of them, though it had taken most of the spring to work up the nerve to actually suggest it.

"Feels like the right time," she said, unpacking the last box, the little house already settling into their shared shape. "Everything's steady now. Dawn's settled, Trelane's part of the furniture, the Keepers aren't looming over every conversation anymore. Feels like a good foundation to build something of our own on top of."

"Yeah," Charlie agreed, watching her arrange a shelf of books that had, gradually, become as much his as hers. "Feels like exactly the right time."

Dawn visited that evening, delighted by the new arrangement in the particular, sincere way she greeted every small milestone in the people she loved. *a home of your own,* she said, warmth spilling through the words. *i am so glad for you both. i have learned this year that homes matter enormously — not the walls, precisely, but what gets built inside them.*

"You'd know," Clara said, smiling. "Better than most, probably."

*perhaps. i have had rather a remarkable first year to learn from.*

Trelane, arriving with his usual theatrical flourish to inspect the new living arrangement, offered his own characteristically overblown blessing. *A proper household, established with genuine intention. How delightfully domestic. I shall have to find you both a suitably impressive housewarming gift.*

"Please don't," Charlie said, immediate and heartfelt.

*Too late. I've already been considering options.*

"Nothing that could level the barn," Millie called from the main house, without even needing to see what was happening to know it needed saying.

*One incident, and I'm never permitted to forget it,* Trelane said, wounded, though the affection underneath the complaint was unmistakable. *Very well. Something modest, then. I do listen, on occasion, when properly instructed.*

The evening rolled on easy and warm, the whole extended, remarkable family gathered loosely around Charlie and Clara's small new home. This, Charlie realized, watching it all unfold — ordinary joy, astonishing family, a summer evening with nothing more dramatic on the agenda than settling into a life well and truly earned — was the future he'd once, quietly, in his most hopeful moments, dared to imagine.

***



Chapter 78: Chapter 78



The first proper Keeper check-in arrived that autumn, a good deal lighter in tone than any previous encounter, arriving almost casually into an ordinary evening rather than announcing itself as a significant event.

*we thought we would simply see how things are proceeding,* the presence said, settling into the field with none of its earlier vast, careful weight. *no verdict required this time. merely curiosity.*

"That's new," Fury observed, dry, though plainly more relaxed than he'd been at any previous encounter.

*we are learning, ourselves, how to simply visit rather than only evaluate. it is a newer skill for us than you might expect, given our age.* Something almost like warmth threaded through the vast consideration. *the elder child seems well.*

*I am thriving, actually,* Trelane put in, arriving with his usual flourish, though something completely fond colored his greeting toward the presence. *I've taken up cards. And Shakespeare. And an entirely unreasonable fondness for a household that insists on feeding me despite my having no actual need for food. I find I don't mind the unreasonableness one bit.*

*good,* the Keepers said, simple, carrying more feeling in the single word than Charlie would have thought such vast beings capable of expressing. *and the younger child.*

*i am well too,* Dawn said, warmth spilling easily through her words now, months of practice making the exchange feel natural rather than momentous. *i have a home. a family, twice over now. i am learning new things nearly every day, and i find i am rarely afraid of any of it.*

*that is precisely what we hoped to hear.* A pause, something almost like true parental satisfaction settling into the words. *we will not stay long today. we only wished to see for ourselves that the hope we felt, watching your last year unfold, remains justified.*

"It does," Millie said, simple and certain. "Every day, it does."

*then we are glad,* the Keepers said, already beginning to withdraw, the visit ending as gently as it had begun. *live well, both of you. we will return again, in time. simply to visit, as you say. nothing more urgent than that.*

The presence faded, the field settling back into ordinary autumn quiet, and the household exchanged warm, easy glances — no crisis this time, no verdict hanging heavy over the conversation, just the simple, painstaking pleasure of a family, however unconventional, checking in on itself with nothing more complicated than honest care.

"That was nice," Willow said, visiting for the weekend, something soft and pleased in her voice. "Just... nice. Feels like a good sign, honestly, that the scariest beings in this whole story are getting the hang of casual visits."

"Feels like exactly the shape of how this was always supposed to go, eventually," Millie agreed, watching the last of the light settle back into its familiar, steady glow above the field. "Took us all a while to get here. Worth every bit of the wait."

***



Chapter 79: Chapter 79



Joe proposed on a Tuesday, which surprised absolutely no one who knew him well enough to recognize the exact, deliberate practicality of the choice — no grand gesture, no elaborate planning, just the two of them out by the workshop after a long day, Cent halfway through locking up when Joe simply set a small, unassuming ring box on the workbench between them.

"I've been carrying that around for three weeks," he admitted, something nervous and hopeful in his voice despite his usual steady composure. "Kept waiting for the right moment. Then I realized we've had about a hundred right moments already this year, and I was just overthinking the delivery."

Cent stared at the box, then at him, something bright and disbelieving breaking across her face. "Are you—"

"Marry me," Joe said, simple, direct, exactly the way he said everything that mattered most. "Not because of some grand romantic timeline. Just because I've spent years now building a life alongside you that I don't want to imagine building with anyone else. Figured it was time to actually say that properly instead of just living it quietly."

Cent didn't answer with words at first, just crossed the space between them and kissed him, thorough and certain, years of easy, established devotion condensing into a single, overwhelming moment of clarity.

"Yes," she said, finally, breathless. "Obviously yes. You didn't even need the ring, honestly, though I'm very glad you got one."

"Wanted to do it properly. Even if the delivery was apparently underwhelming by your account."

"It wasn't underwhelming. It was exactly us. Practical and quiet and completely, perfectly right." Cent kissed him again, laughing now, joy spilling over in a way that felt, after everything this past year had held, entirely, gloriously earned.

Marie found them a few minutes later, still glowing, and let out a delighted whoop the second Cent held up her hand. "Finally! I've been waiting for this since Christmas, you absolute coward," she said to Joe, already pulling both of them into a hug. "You told me you had a plan back in March."

"I had several plans. This was the seventh one."

"You could've used any of the first six."

"They weren't right yet."

Word spread through the household within the hour, the whole family gathering with sincere, unrestrained delight, Millie crying openly, Davy shaking Joe's hand with something close to actual pride, Dawn's light flaring bright and joyful above the celebration.

*this is wonderful,* Dawn said, warmth spilling through every word. *i did not know a proposal could feel this way to witness. i am so glad i got to be here for it.*

"Wouldn't have wanted it any other way," Cent said, glowing, Joe's hand steady in hers, Marie already talking Millie's ear off about engagement party logistics nearby. "You're all family. Course you'd be here."

The evening rolled into celebration, easy and warm, one more piece of ordinary, well-earned joy folded into a year that had, against every reasonable expectation, made a true habit of them.

***



Chapter 80: Chapter 80



Faith's own quiet milestone arrived a few weeks later, a great deal less dramatic in its unfolding but no less significant to the people who noticed it — the day she finally, fully stopped flinching when someone mentioned the future in front of her.

Willow caught it first, at lunch, Buffy idly mentioning summer plans a full year out without Faith's usual careful deflection following close behind.

"You didn't do the thing," Willow observed, watching her carefully.

"What thing."

"The thing where you change the subject whenever anybody talks about anything more than a few months away. You've done it for years. Just now, you didn't."

Faith considered that, something thoughtful crossing her face as she turned the observation over properly. "Huh. Guess I didn't." She held the moment a beat longer, true surprise settling into something warmer. "Feels like I finally believe I'll actually be here for it, I guess. The future. Didn't used to feel like a safe bet, believing that. Feels different now."

"Good different?"

"Really good different." Faith glanced toward Xander, toward Buffy, the easy, established shape of the family she'd built here over the past two years. "Guess somewhere along the way, I stopped bracing for the other shoe. Don't know exactly when it happened. Just noticed, today, that it had."

Buffy reached over, squeezing her hand, something proud and unguarded in the gesture. "That's a good thing to notice."

"Yeah." Faith managed an honest, easy smile, much lighter than her usual careful bravado. "Kinda is, actually."

The table settled back into its familiar rhythm, but Xander caught Willow's eye across it, both of them recognizing, without needing to say it aloud, the quiet significance of what had just passed — one more piece of dearly bought healing, arriving not with fanfare, but with the simple, steady relief of someone finally, fully trusting that good things were allowed to last.

***



Chapter 81: Chapter 81



The engagement party happened three weeks later, Marie having taken over the planning with the same meticulous enthusiasm she brought to satellite trajectory work, and the homestead filled once more with the particular warm chaos of a household that had gotten remarkably good at celebrating things properly.

Joe's own family drove in from two states over, significantly more overwhelmed by the scale of the gathering than they let on, his mother pulling Cent aside within the first hour with the precise, searching look of someone determining, for herself, whether her son had chosen well.

"He talks about you like you hung the moon," she said, watching Cent's face carefully. "Has for years now. I wanted to see for myself what put that look in his eyes."

"I could say the same about him, honestly," Cent said. "He's steady. Kind. Doesn't flinch at any of—" she gestured vaguely toward Dawn's distant light, toward the whole incredible shape of her family's life, "—any of this. Just shows up, every day, exactly himself."

"That's the boy I raised," his mother said, something gentle and satisfied settling into her expression. "Glad to see the world hasn't managed to change that part of him, whatever else it's apparently thrown his way this year."

Joe found Charlie near the drinks table, far more relaxed than he'd been at the start of the party, tie already loosened. "Your mom cornered mine yet?"

"About twenty minutes ago. Looked intense for a second. Seems to have resolved fine."

"Good. I was mostly worried about the part where somebody tries to explain Dawn to her in casual party conversation. Feels like a lot to drop on someone between appetizers."

"We kept it vague. 'Family friend, kind of a big deal, glows a little' seemed to cover it without inviting follow-up questions we're not ready for."

Joe laughed, honest and easy. "Efficient. I like it."

Marie found them both a while later, a good deal pleased with how the party had come together, dragging Joe off toward the dance floor she'd insisted on setting up despite his mild protests about his own dancing abilities.

"You don't have to be good at it," she informed him, firmly. "You just have to do it, because your fiancée's over there watching and she deserves to see you try."

Cent, watching from across the yard as Joe allowed himself to be pulled along, laughing despite himself, felt something soft and complete settle into place — this life, this family, this particular, painstaking ordinary joy, exactly as full and exactly as striking as she'd never quite dared hope it could become.

***



Chapter 82: Chapter 82



Trelane took to attending the party circuit with obvious delight, and by winter had become something of a fixture at every homestead gathering, his theatrical flourishes gradually softening, over months, into something that read less like performance and more like simply, truly, enjoying being included.

"You've gotten quieter," Giles observed, one evening, watching him hold a great deal less court than his usual habit. "Not less present. Just — less performed about it."

*Have I?* Trelane considered the observation with plain thoughtfulness. *I hadn't noticed, honestly. I suppose I no longer feel the need to earn my welcome quite so loudly. It's a strange thing, being wanted without having to perform for it. Took me rather longer to trust than I expected.*

"That's not a small thing to have learned."

*No. I don't believe it is.* Something easy and settled had replaced his earlier restless theatricality. *I find I like this version of myself much better than the one who required an audience simply to feel real. Dawn taught me that, I think, without ever meaning to teach anyone anything. She simply existed, glad and unguarded, and I found myself wanting to match it.*

Dawn, listening nearby, pulsed warm with obvious affection. *you give me too much credit. i think you did that yourself.*

*Perhaps. I'll allow you partial credit regardless. Sisters are entitled to some.*

The evening rolled on easy and warm, Trelane settling into quieter corners of conversation rather than commanding every one of them, Giles matching him book for book in a running literary debate that had, by now, become one of the household's most reliable sources of gentle amusement, and Charlie, watching it all unfold, thought that this — a once-dangerous, once-lonely being finding sincere peace in the same, unglamorous work of simply belonging somewhere — might be the quietest, truest kind of wonder this whole remarkable year had offered.

***



Chapter 83: Chapter 83



Willow found the pattern almost by accident, deep into a quiet winter evening of what had become, over the past year, an ongoing personal research project — cross-referencing Dawn's energy signature against every mystical text she could get her hands on, partly out of genuine scholarly curiosity, partly because the unresolved question of Dawn and Buffy's connection had never quite stopped nagging at her.

"Okay, I think I found something," she said, calling Buffy the second the pattern clicked into place. "It's not conclusive. But it's interesting enough that I didn't want to sit on it."

"Tell me."

"There's an old concept in some of the more obscure mystical texts — energy that recognizes its own eventual shape before it's fully formed. Not prophecy, exactly. More like... resonance across time, two points of the same continuum recognizing each other out of sequence." Willow's voice carried the particular, careful excitement of someone handling a honestly significant idea gently. "I think that might be what's happening between you and Dawn. Not that you're connected now because of something that already happened. Connected because of something that hasn't happened yet, that you're both already, somehow, feeling the shape of."

Buffy took that in, something settling into place that felt less like surprise and more like confirmation of something she'd half-known all along. "That's a really strange thing to have you tell me on a Tuesday."

"Yeah, sorry, no good way to ease into interdimensional resonance theory." Willow's tone gentled. "You don't have to do anything with this. I just didn't want to keep it to myself, given how much this connection's clearly meant to both of you."

"No, I'm glad you told me." Buffy looked out toward the horizon, toward the distant, unknowable shape of whatever future Willow's theory suggested was already, somehow, reaching backward to find her. "Feels like it explains something I never had words for. Just — knew, the second I met her. That whatever this was, it mattered more than a single afternoon usually would."

"You gonna tell her? What I found?"

Buffy considered the question seriously. "Not yet. I don't want to hand her a theory about her own future before she's had the chance to just be young and new and figuring herself out first. Feels like that's hers to discover, eventually, on her own terms, not mine to hand her early."

"That's probably wise," Willow agreed. "I'll keep researching quietly. No rush."

"No rush," Buffy echoed, something gentle and steady settling into the words. "Whatever this actually is, whenever it actually resolves — I get the feeling it's gonna be worth the wait."

***



Chapter 84: Chapter 84



Spring brought its own quiet developments, the household settling into its second full year with Dawn now grown enough — conceptually, if not physically — to ask increasingly sophisticated questions about the wider world beyond the field she'd first been born into.

*i would like to see more of it,* she told Millie, one warm April evening. *not just through descriptions, or through visitors bringing pieces of it to me. i think i am ready to actually go, if that is possible.*

Millie considered the request with true care, old protective instinct weighing against the true reasonableness of it. "How would that even work? You've never left the field."

*i do not fully know. but shuri and jane have theories, and wong believes it may be possible now, given how much more settled my own form has become.* Dawn's light pulsed with quiet, hopeful determination. *i do not want to stay contained forever, even by something as gentle as this field. i think i am ready to see what the rest of the world i helped build actually looks like, up close.*

The household gathered to discuss it properly, careful and thorough as always, Natasha and T'Challa working through security implications, Shuri and Jane through the technical mechanics, Wanda through the emotional and mystical considerations that mattered just as much as either.

"I think we try it," Wanda said, finally. "Carefully. Small steps. But I think she's earned the chance to actually ask for this, and I think we owe her an honest answer instead of just reflexive caution."

"I agree," Millie said, something proud and nervous tangled together in her voice. "She's not a secret to protect anymore. She's a person, growing, the same as any of us did. People are allowed to want to see the world."

The first trip happened a week later — nothing dramatic, just a careful, guided visit to the nearest town, Dawn's presence gathered into a form significantly more contained and mobile than her usual open-field glow, Wanda and Millie both close beside her the entire time.

*it's so much,* Dawn said, taking in the ordinary bustle of a small-town main street with open, delighted wonder. *so many people, living so many separate, ordinary lives, none of them knowing anything about the last two years at all. i find that remarkable. i think i love it, actually. all this life, happening everywhere, all the time, whether or not i'm there to witness it.*

"Welcome to the world," Millie said, smiling, watching her daughter experience something as simple as a sunny afternoon on Main Street with the same open wonder she brought to everything. "It's a good one, mostly. Glad you're finally getting to see it properly."

***



Chapter 85: Chapter 85



Dawn's trips into the wider world became a regular fixture over the following months, each one a little further afield than the last, Natasha and T'Challa's careful security scaling alongside her growing confidence — a proper museum visit, a baseball game that left her delighted and thoroughly confused by the rules in equal measure, a quiet evening at a diner that became, unexpectedly, one of her favorite outings yet.

"I don't fully understand pie," she admitted, to Millie, over her third slice in as many visits. "I don't need to eat, technically. But I find I want to, whenever it's this. Is that strange?"

"Not strange at all," Millie said, delighted by the observation. "Pie makes very little logical sense as a food group. Doesn't stop it being wonderful."

"Then I approve of the illogic entirely."

The wider world, gradually, began to know her too — not as a secret or a phenomenon, but as simply, increasingly, a presence in the world's periphery, glimpsed occasionally, always gentle, always careful, the story of her existence settling, over the two years since the disclosure, into something closer to accepted fact than ongoing controversy.

Okoye, visiting for the diner outing, watched Dawn navigate the small, ordinary pleasures of an evening out with plain, quiet satisfaction. "I remember writing my very first report about you," she said. "Cautious, careful, uncertain what to actually recommend. I don't think I could have imagined this. Something this settled. This simply, wonderfully normal."

*i think that is my favorite thing i have learned,* Dawn said, warmth spilling through the words. *that astonishing and normal are not opposites. i can be both, at once, and neither one has to shrink to make room for the other.*

"That's a good lesson," Okoye agreed. "Better than most people twice your age manage to learn."

"She's had good teachers," Millie said, proud, watching her daughter enjoy her pie with uncomplicated, thorough delight. "Whole family full of them, by now."

The evening rolled on easy and warm, one more ordinary outing folded into an incredible life that had, over two remarkable years, made an honest, well-earned habit of finding room for everything that mattered — family, wonder, pie, and the simple, steady joy of a world that had, slowly but surely, learned to make space for something new.

***



Chapter 86: Chapter 86



The wedding planning began in earnest that summer, Marie having appointed herself lead coordinator with an enthusiasm that bordered on gleeful, and the homestead filled, over the following months, with the particular warm chaos of a household organizing itself around sincere, unhurried joy.

"Small," Cent insisted, for what felt like the fifth time that week. "I keep saying small, and every time I check in, the guest list's grown by another dozen people."

"It's not my fault everyone loves you," Marie said, entirely unrepentant, cross-referencing seating charts with the same rigor she usually reserved for orbital mechanics. "Also, in my defense, half the growth is just people asking if Dawn's coming, and then their whole extended families wanting to come specifically to meet her."

"That's an unmistakable phenomenon now, apparently. Dawn-adjacent wedding tourism."

"I did not invent it. I am simply managing it responsibly."

Joe, arriving with two coffees and the exact weary affection of a groom who'd learned, months ago, that the actual wedding planning had very little to do with him, handed one to Cent and settled beside her. "How bad is it today?"

"Guest list's at ninety now."

"That's not small."

"I'm aware."

"I'm not complaining," Joe said, easy. "Just observing. Ninety people who love you enough to want to be there. Feels like a good problem to have, honestly, even if it's technically a problem."

Dawn, visiting for the planning session, offered her own careful input when consulted, delighted by the whole novel process of wedding logistics. *i would like to help, if it is wanted. i have never planned a celebration before. i find the idea rather thrilling.*

"You're helping just by being excited about it," Cent said, warm. "Though if you want an actual job, Marie could probably use backup on the seating chart. She's been at war with the Hendersons' table placement for two days."

*i would be glad to help with that. diplomacy seems like exactly the kind of skill i should practice more.*

The planning rolled on through the summer, unhurried and entirely joyful, ninety guests slowly, carefully organized into a celebration that promised, by every indication, to be exactly as full and exactly as warm as the family building it.

***



Chapter 87: Chapter 87



Charlie's own quiet milestone arrived that same summer, far less dramatic than a wedding but no less significant to him — his father finally, formally handing over full responsibility for the strip's maintenance, the small ceremony of it happening over nothing more elaborate than a shared cup of coffee in the hangar.

"Feels strange," Charlie admitted, looking over the logbook his father had kept meticulously for over two decades. "Taking this over properly. Feels like a bigger thing than just fuel caching and runway grading."

"It is a bigger thing," his father said, simple. "Not because the work's harder. Because it means I trust you to carry something I've carried a long time, without needing to check behind you constantly. That's not nothing."

"You'll still fly with me, though. Right?"

"Every chance I get. This isn't retirement. Just — passing the actual weight of it over, properly, the way it's supposed to happen eventually." His father set a weathered hand on Charlie's shoulder. "Your Uncle Max would be proud, watching this. I believe that more than I believe most things these days."

Charlie felt the old grief settle, warm now instead of heavy, fully at peace in a way it had taken two remarkable years to properly earn.

"I'll take care of it," he said. "All of it. The strips, the mission, whatever else this family ends up carrying, going forward."

"I know you will," his father said. "You already have been, if you look at the last two years honestly. This is just making it official."

They finished their coffee in comfortable quiet, the hangar settling around them with its familiar smell of avgas and old machinery, and Charlie thought, looking out at the strip stretching gold in the summer morning light, that this — quiet responsibility, freely given and freely accepted, one generation's careful work handed forward to the next — was its own kind of wonder, no less genuine for being so thoroughly, gently ordinary.

***



Chapter 88: Chapter 88



The wedding itself arrived on a clear October afternoon, the homestead transformed for the day into something between its ordinary working self and a true celebration ground, ninety guests settling into borrowed chairs arranged across the same open field that had, two years earlier, first held a newborn light's uncertain first hello.

Charlie stood near the front with the rest of the wedding party, watching the gathered crowd with quiet, true amazement at the shape of it — family by blood and family by choice tangled together so thoroughly now that the distinction barely mattered anymore. Thor and Jane, seated together, Vision's steady presence beside Wanda, Trelane holding court with a cluster of delighted, curious guests who'd clearly never met anyone quite like him before, Giles deep in what looked like a plainly engrossing conversation with Joe's grandmother about, of all things, Elizabethan theater.

Dawn hovered near the aisle's edge, gathered into her most solid, most careful form yet, warm light settled into something that read, at a respectful distance, almost like a person standing quietly among the guests.

*i am so happy,* she told Millie, quiet, as the ceremony's opening notes began. *i did not know it was possible to feel this full. i think i understand, now, why humans make such a no small amount of event of these things.*

"Wait till you see her walk down that aisle," Millie said, squeezing her hand. "This is nothing yet."

Cent appeared at the field's edge a few minutes later, and the whole gathered crowd rose together, and Charlie watched Joe's face transform, watching her, into something so plainly, thoroughly overcome that several guests audibly teared up just from witnessing it secondhand.

The ceremony itself unfolded simple and warm, vows exchanged with the same steady, deliberate honesty that had carried both of them through two remarkable years, and when the officiant finally pronounced them married, the whole field erupted into honest, unrestrained joy — Dawn's light flaring bright and radiant above the celebration, Trelane's applause a good deal more theatrical than strictly necessary, the whole striking, ordinary family of it gathered together in a single, overwhelming moment of shared happiness.

"We did it," Cent said, later, dancing with Joe under string lights Rick had spent three days hanging, both of them glowing with the particular exhausted joy of a day gone exactly right. "Actually did it. Married. Feels surreal."

"Feels earned," Joe said, simple, pulling her close. "Every single bit of it."

Millie found Davy near the edge of the dance floor, watching their daughter's happiness with the precise, overwhelming pride that had been building for two decades now, finally, fully realized.

"We did alright," she said, leaning into him. "Whatever else this life turned out to hold — we did alright, raising her."

"We did," Davy agreed, pulling her close, watching Cent laugh, bright and unguarded, in the arms of a man who plainly adored her. "Better than alright, honestly. Look at all of it. Everything we built, everyone we found along the way. I don't think either of us could've imagined this, starting out."

They stood together a while longer, watching the celebration unfold around them, and above it all, Dawn's light pulsed soft and steady and thoroughly, unmistakably glad — one more piece of dearly bought, ordinary joy folded into a family that had, against every reasonable expectation, learned to make room for absolutely everything that mattered.

***



Chapter 89: Chapter 89



Winter returned to the homestead with its now-familiar quiet, the household settling into its third year with Dawn grown enough, by now, that the household had largely stopped thinking of her age in months at all — simply present, simply family, the same wonder of her existence woven so thoroughly into ordinary life that it rarely announced itself anymore.

Ashgrove, home for the season in the fullest sense now, found Millie in the kitchen one snowy evening, both of them nursing tea in comfortable, companionable quiet.

"I had a thought today," Ashgrove said, eventually. "Watching Dawn help Cent address wedding thank-you cards, of all the mundane things. I found myself thinking, quite plainly, that I could not imagine my life without this family in it anymore. That felt worth saying aloud."

"I feel the same," Millie said, warm. "Every day, honestly. Strange, how thoroughly a satellite anomaly turned into this."

"Strange and wonderful in equal measure." Ashgrove considered her tea a long moment. "I've been in contact with Home's Council again, incidentally. Nothing urgent. They've asked whether this family would be willing to help advise other emergent cases, should any ever occur elsewhere. Given how thoroughly you've all navigated this one."

Millie considered that, surprised by the request but not, she found, opposed to it. "Other families going through something like this?"

"Potentially. Dawn's emergence remains unique, but the Keepers have made clear it may not remain unprecedented forever. Should another case arise, Home would like this family's perspective available, if you're willing to offer it."

"I'd want to talk to Davy. To Charlie, Cent, everyone, really. Not just decide alone." Millie considered it further, something thoughtful and completely open in her expression. "But my instinct's already leaning yes. If there's ever another family standing where we stood two years ago, scared and uncertain — I'd want them to have somebody who actually understood, rather than facing it as alone as we nearly did."

"That's precisely the spirit Home was hoping for," Ashgrove said, something proud in her voice. "I'll relay it, once you've had the fuller conversation."

They lingered at the table, the snow falling soft and steady outside the kitchen window. Millie watched it settle over the quiet, familiar contours of the property and let herself hope: the possibility of their painstaking wisdom someday helping another family through the same uncertain wonder felt like the right way for this whole remarkable story to keep growing outward, generous and patient, the same way everything good in this family's life had always, eventually, managed to grow.

***



Chapter 90: Chapter 90



The household gathered properly to discuss Ashgrove's proposal a week later, the whole extended family present — Charlie and Clara, Cent and Joe back from their brief honeymoon, Marie glowing with her own recent engagement to a colleague from Shuri's research team that had developed, quietly, over the past year of collaboration.

"I think we should do it," Charlie said, once the proposal had been laid out properly. "Not because we owe Home anything. Because I remember how scared we were, that first month, not knowing if any of this would turn out okay. If we can spare another family that fear, even partly, feels like exactly the kind of thing worth doing."

"I agree," Cent said. "Feels like the actual shape of what Mom and Dad always taught us the mission was supposed to be about. Not just watching. Helping, when helping's actually possible."

Davy, who'd carried the most wariness through the whole conversation, finally spoke, something settled and certain in his voice that surprised even himself. "I keep thinking about Conley's basement. About eleven days not knowing if anyone was even looking for me. I don't want anyone else facing something that big, that uncertain, without somebody in their corner who's actually been through it." He reached for Millie's hand. "I think I'm ready to be that somebody, if it's ever needed. Been a long time coming, but I think I finally am."

Millie felt her chest tighten with old, complicated love, watching him say it — the man who'd once needed years just to trust his own safety, now offering himself as a steady hand for someone else's uncertain future.

"Then we do it," she said, simple. "Together, the way we've done everything else this family's ever faced."

Dawn, listening with quiet attention, offered her own thoughtful addition. *i would like to help too, if another emergence ever happens. i think i would understand, better than almost anyone, what such a being might need to hear in their very first hours.*

"We'll make sure you're included," Millie promised. "Whatever this ends up looking like, however it unfolds — you're part of this family's answer to it, same as the rest of us."

The household gave way to agreement, easy and unified, one more piece of the family's ever-expanding purpose folded into a life that had, across two remarkable years, learned to turn even its hardest-won wisdom into something generous, something offered freely outward, exactly the way everything good about this family had always, eventually, chosen to grow.

***



Chapter 91: Chapter 91



Marie's own engagement, mentioned almost in passing during the family meeting, got its properly celebrated due the following weekend — a much smaller gathering than Cent's wedding had warranted, but no less warm for its modest size, the whole household delighted to fold one more piece of well-earned happiness into the family's growing collection.

"His name's Dr. Adaeze Nwosu," Marie explained, to Faith, who'd come out for the occasion. "Materials scientist. Met her through Shuri's research team eighteen months ago, and somehow neither of us noticed we were falling for each other until we were already several months deep into it."

"Sounds like a very you way to fall in love," Faith said, grinning. "Slow, thorough, backed by a good deal of research."

"I resent the accuracy of that observation."

"I mean it as a compliment. Mostly." Faith bumped her shoulder against Marie's, easy and fond. "Glad you found somebody, though. Truly. You've been the quiet backbone of about half this family's biggest moments this past two years. Deserve some happiness of your own to show for it."

Adaeze herself, meeting the extended family properly for the first time, took the whole astonishing scope of it — Dawn's warm presence, Trelane's theatrical greeting, the general accumulated strangeness of two years' worth of cosmic development — with the same careful, delighted curiosity she apparently brought to her actual research.

"Marie undersold this much," she told Millie, watching Dawn help Cent's new mother-in-law navigate a particularly enthusiastic greeting from Trelane. "She called it 'a bit unusual.' This is rather more than a bit."

"We get that a lot," Millie said, warm. "Welcome to it, though. Once you're properly folded in, it stops feeling unusual and starts just feeling like family."

"I believe I'm looking forward to that, actually." Adaeze watched the gathered chaos with sincere affection. "Marie speaks of all of you constantly. I confess I wondered if any family could really live up to that much fondness. I think I finally understand why it does."

The celebration rolled on gentle and easy, one more piece of the family's steadily expanding joy settling into place. Watching it unfold, Charlie decided this — new people finding their way in, gently, gladly, into something already so full of love — might be the truest, steadiest kind of wonder this whole incredible story had to offer.

***



Chapter 92: Chapter 92



Spring arrived again, the third since Marie's original discovery in the workshop, and the household marked the anniversary quietly, gathering in the same field where it had all begun, significantly changed from the tense, uncertain crowd that had first assembled there.

"Three years," Millie said, looking around at the gathered family — smaller than the wedding, larger than the earliest days, exactly the right size for a moment meant to be felt rather than performed. "Feels impossible, some days, how far we've come from that first night."

*i remember it clearly,* Dawn said, warmth threading through the words. *the fear in the room, underneath all the careful planning. i felt it, even before i had words for feelings. i am glad i got to watch it turn into this instead.*

Trelane, far more settled now than his earlier theatrical self, offered his own quiet addition. *I remember arriving to find a family already so full of love that I worried, briefly, there wouldn't be room for me. I was wrong to worry. There is always room, apparently, in this particular family. I've stopped being surprised by it.*

"There's always room," Millie agreed. "That was true before any of this started. I think it's just gotten easier to actually prove, the bigger this family's gotten."

Ashgrove raised her glass, the old toast worn comfortable now with three years of repetition. "To three years. And to however many more this family chooses to build, together, from here."

"To three years," the gathered crowd echoed, soft and easy, and Charlie, watching the whole striking, ordinary shape of it — family found and family chosen, wonder and grief and joy all woven together into something durable, something honest — thought that whatever came next, however this particular story continued to unfold, it had already, thoroughly, proven itself worth every uncertain, hopeful step that had brought them here.

Above them all, Dawn's light pulsed easy and steady, unhurried as it had always been, glad as it had always been, watching over a family that had taught her, from her very first hello, exactly what it meant to belong somewhere, freely, completely, for as long as it took.

***



Chapter 93: Chapter 93



The call came through Ashgrove's channel on an otherwise ordinary Tuesday morning, and Charlie knew from the particular speed with which she crossed the kitchen, tablet already in hand, that whatever it was couldn't wait for a more convenient moment.

"Home's just received word," she said, gathering the household with a glance. "Another emergence. Not identical to Dawn's — the conditions differ in ways we're still working to understand — but close enough in shape that the Council's asking for this family's involvement immediately, rather than waiting for a formal request to work through channels."

"Where," Millie asked, already moving toward practicality, old instinct steadying her voice.

"A different threshold entirely. Not a gate like ours — a different kind of doorway, maintained by a family in rural Manitoba for nearly as long as ours maintained this one, though under circumstances I don't yet fully understand." Ashgrove paused, choosing her next words with visible care. "I should clarify — this is not our Manitoba.
