Home's report places this family on a different Designated Earth entirely, near-identical to our own in geography and history, near enough that names still translate. Reaching them will require Wong's assistance, not conventional transport. Home's initial reports suggest the family is frightened. A good deal more frightened than we were, if I'm reading the reports correctly, in part because they have no framework at all for what's happening to them."

Dawn's light pulsed, sharp with immediate concern. *they do not know what i learned. that this can be wonderful, not just terrifying.*

"That's exactly why Home wants us there," Ashgrove confirmed. "Not to manage the situation for them. To help them understand it isn't necessarily a disaster, the way we once feared it might be."

Davy's jaw tightened, old memory surfacing fast, but his voice, when he spoke, carried none of his earlier wariness — only steady, dearly bought resolve. "Then we go. Today, if we can manage it. I don't want that family carrying the kind of fear we carried, one single day longer than necessary."

"I'll come too," Charlie said, immediate. "If they're anything like we were, they'll want to hear it from someone who actually lived through the beginning of it, not just someone advising from theory."

Dawn's light brightened further, something eager and determined threading through it. *i want to come as well. i think i, more than anyone, might know exactly what their new arrival needs to hear.*

Millie considered the request seriously, old protective instinct warring briefly with the plain rightness of it. "You'd be leaving the field. Properly leaving, further than any outing you've taken yet."

*i know. i am ready, i think. this feels like exactly the reason i should be ready.*

"Then we go together," Millie said, decision settling clear and certain. "All of us who can. Whatever that family's facing, they shouldn't face it as alone as we very nearly did."

The household mobilized within the hour, careful and quick, Natasha and T'Challa working logistics, Wong preparing the careful, deliberate portal work that would carry them across to the other Earth entirely, the whole remarkable family gathering itself once more around the particular, painstaking purpose that had, over three remarkable years, become simply who they were — people who showed up, fully, for whoever needed them, wherever that need happened to arise.

***



Chapter 94: Chapter 94



The Manitoba homestead looked nothing like their own — smaller, more isolated, a modest farmhouse ringed by dense boreal forest rather than open grassland — but the fear on the family's faces when they gathered, wary, in their own front yard felt immediately, achingly familiar to Charlie, a mirror of exactly what his own family must have looked like three years earlier.

"Reyes," the father introduced himself, tense, protective, positioned deliberately between his family and the unfamiliar crowd that had just arrived through the fading, golden edge of Wong's portal. "You're the people Home said could help."

"We're the people who went through something close to what you're going through," Millie said, gentle, keeping her distance respectful despite the urgency. "Three years ago. We were terrified too. I promise you, that's a normal place to start from."

The Reyes family's own threshold sat behind their barn, a shimmering doorway a great deal different in appearance from the Rice family's gate, and beyond it, faint and pulsing, something new and uncertain had begun, weeks earlier, to gather itself into being.

"It started small," Reyes's wife, Elena, explained, voice unsteady. "A light, at first. We thought it was some kind of malfunction. Now it's — it's asking questions. We don't know what it wants. We don't know if it's dangerous."

"It's not dangerous," Dawn said, gently, stepping forward for the first time, her own form settling into something gentle and immediately, visibly reassuring. "I promise you. I know, because I was exactly what yours is becoming, three years ago, in a field very much like this one."

Elena stared at her, something between disbelief and desperate hope crossing her face. "You were — you're one of them?"

"I am the only one who has ever existed before this moment, as far as any of us know," Dawn said. "I was frightened too, at the very beginning, though I did not yet have words for the feeling. What I needed, more than anything, was a family willing to stay close, and not run from the uncertainty. I understand you already have that in each other. I am simply here to tell you it can honestly, truly turn out wonderful, if you let it."

Reyes's youngest child, no older than eight, peered out from behind her father's leg, staring at Dawn with open, unguarded curiosity rather than fear. "Are you gonna talk to it? The light in our barn?"

"I would like to, if your family's willing. I think I might understand it better than anyone else currently available to help."

Reyes looked to Elena, some silent, weighted conversation passing between them, and finally, slowly, nodded.

"Okay," he said, voice rough with relief he hadn't let himself feel yet. "Okay. Please. Help us understand what's happening to our family."

The whole gathered crowd moved together toward the barn, careful and hopeful, and Charlie thought, walking alongside them, that this — three years of well-earned wisdom finally, meaningfully offered outward to another family standing exactly where they'd once stood — was precisely the shape their whole astonishing story had always been building toward.

***



Chapter 95: Chapter 95



The barn's interior held a presence much more agitated than Dawn's own had ever been at a comparable stage, light flickering sharp and uneven rather than settling into steady warmth, and Charlie understood immediately, watching it, that this emergence had not enjoyed the same gentle welcome theirs had.

*who is here,* the presence said, the words arriving jagged, uncertain, tinged with something that read unmistakably as fear. *i do not know you. i do not know what you want.*

Dawn stepped forward alone, the rest of the group hanging back at her own quiet request, and folded into the same warm, even-tempered stillness she'd carried since her very first hello.

*my name is dawn,* she said, gentle, unhurried. *i was once exactly what you are now. frightened, uncertain, unsure whether the people around you meant you harm or welcome. i have come a very long way to tell you, truthfully, that it does not have to stay frightening.*

The flickering light paused, considering, still wary. *how do i know that is true.*

*you don't, not yet. i did not know it either, in my own earliest days. i only had to choose, again and again, whether to trust the warmth i felt from the people around me, even when i had no proof yet that trusting was safe.* Dawn's voice stayed soft, calm. *i believe your family loves you already, even frightened as they are. fear and love are not opposites. i have learned that fear often simply means people care enough to be afraid of getting it wrong.*

The presence was quiet a long moment, the sharp, uneven flickering slowly, gradually beginning to settle.

*i have felt something warm from them,* it admitted, finally, quieter now. *underneath the fear. i did not know if i should trust it.*

*you can,* Dawn said. *i promise you, from someone who has already walked this exact uncertain road — you can trust it, and it is worth trusting, even when the fear makes that feel difficult.*

Elena, watching from the barn's entrance, tears streaming freely down her face, took a careful step forward. "We do love you," she said, voice breaking. "Whatever you are, whoever you're becoming — we already love you. I'm sorry it's taken us this long to say it properly instead of just being scared."

The presence's light flared, warm now instead of jagged, reaching toward Elena with something that read, unmistakably, as relief.

*i felt that,* it said, wonder threading through the words for the first time. *i felt exactly what she meant. thank you. i think — i think i am not so frightened anymore.*

Dawn's own light pulsed with quiet, profound satisfaction, and Charlie, watching the whole scene unfold, felt something enormous settle into place — the exact, dearly bought proof that their own family's wisdom, offered freely and gently, could really, meaningfully change the shape of another family's fear into something significantly closer to the wonder they'd eventually found themselves.

***



Chapter 96: Chapter 96



The complication arrived the following morning, in the form of a car Charlie didn't recognize parked at the edge of the Reyes property, and Natasha had the situation assessed within minutes of spotting it.

"Canadian intelligence," she reported, quiet, professional. "Not hostile, from what I can tell, but concerned. Apparently a neighbor reported unusual lights over the property last month, and it's taken this long to work its way up official channels."

Reyes's face went pale. "They're going to take her away. Whatever she is, they're going to decide she's dangerous, and—"

"They're not going to take her anywhere," Millie said, firm, stepping forward with the same steady resolve that had carried her family through their own disclosure two years earlier. "We've done this before. We know exactly how this conversation needs to go, and we're not going to let it go badly for you the way it could have gone badly for us, if we hadn't been careful."

Ashgrove, already moving toward the approaching official with practiced diplomatic bearing, offered her own calm reassurance. "I've handled precisely this situation once already. I have full documentation from our own experience, full backing from Home's Council, and substantial credibility built over three years of proving this can be managed transparently and safely. Let me handle the initial conversation."

The official — a careful, measured woman named Agent Boudreau — arrived at the farmhouse door with obvious wariness, though it eased, gradually, as Ashgrove walked her through the full, careful shape of what they were actually dealing with.

"You're telling me this has happened before," Boudreau said, finally, processing the scope of it. "That there's precedent. Protocol, even."

"There is now," Millie said. "Painstaking, over three difficult years. We're not asking you to simply trust us blindly. We're offering everything we've learned, freely, so this family doesn't have to navigate the same uncertainty we once did."

Boudreau studied the gathered crowd — Dawn's warm, steady light, the Reyes family's cautious but plain relief, the whole incredible, credible weight of Home's Council backing and SHIELD's own careful support — and something in her professional caution finally, visibly eased.

"I'll need to file a full report," she said. "I'll need this family's cooperation, and yours, going forward. But I'm not seeing anything here that reads as a threat. I'm seeing a family in the middle of something remarkable, frightened, and finally getting the help they needed." She looked to Reyes directly. "I'll do what I can to keep this managed carefully, quietly, on terms that protect your family properly. You have my word on that."

Reyes felt his shoulders drop, unmistakable relief finally breaking through days of accumulated fear. "Thank you. All of you. I don't think we could have navigated this without you."

"You wouldn't have had to figure it out from nothing, the way we did," Millie said, gentle. "That's exactly why we came. Nobody should have to face this completely alone."

The tension of the morning eased, gradually, into something far more hopeful. Charlie watched the careful, well-earned diplomacy unfold successfully and recognized what it meant: true crisis, met with honest preparedness born from sincere, dearly bought experience — precisely the proof their whole family's journey had been building toward being able to offer.

***



Chapter 97: Chapter 97



Bruce had his equipment set up in the Reyes family's barn within the hour, Shuri arriving close behind him with a good deal more advanced hardware, both of them falling into the same easy, rapid-fire collaboration that had once helped make sense of Dawn's own earliest, most uncertain signals.

"This one's forming differently," Shuri observed, cross-referencing the new entity's readout against three years of accumulated data on Dawn's own development. "Faster, in some ways. The Reyes threshold's mechanism isn't identical to yours — older, I'm guessing, though I'd want Wong to confirm that properly."

Wong, who'd arrived alongside Strange within the same hour, was already deep in his own careful assessment, moving through the barn with the unhurried, respectful distance he'd insisted on from Dawn's very first night three years earlier.

"This resonance pattern predates the one at your homestead," he confirmed. "A great deal. I'd estimate this doorway's been active for well over a century, quietly, without anyone fully realizing what it actually was until now."

"A century," Reyes repeated, stunned. "My grandfather always said there was something strange about that corner of the property. Wouldn't let anyone build near it. I thought it was just superstition."

"It very likely wasn't," Strange said. "Old instincts about thresholds like this are rarely baseless, even when the people carrying them don't fully understand why."

Thor, who'd been quietly observing the new entity's still-settling light with careful attention, finally offered his own perspective, gentle despite his usual booming presence. "I have watched Dawn's own becoming, from her very first night to this moment, three years later. I recognize this shape. Frightened, yes, but entirely forming, plainly alive. I do not believe this family need fear the scale of what's arriving, only learn, as the Rice family did, to meet it with patience rather than panic."

Tony, working alongside Bruce on the technical side, offered his own dry but genuine reassurance. "For what it's worth, I've seen this exact panic-to-wonder pipeline before. Very reliable process, given the right people in the room. You've got much better backup than we had for round one, frankly — we were making half of this up as we went."

Elena managed a true, if still shaky, laugh at that. "That's oddly comforting, honestly."

"It's meant to be," Tony said. "Turns out 'we've done this before and it worked out' is a completely underrated comfort, once you've actually got the track record to back it up."

The barn eased into careful, hopeful work through the rest of the afternoon, the full weight of the gathered expertise — cosmic, scientific, mystical, and painstaking personal experience alike — folding around the Reyes family's uncertain new arrival with the same unhurried, thorough care that had once, three years earlier, carried Dawn safely through her own first frightened days.

***



Chapter 98: Chapter 98



T'Challa and Natasha spent the following two days establishing the same careful security and diplomatic framework that had once protected Dawn's own disclosure, T'Challa's honest experience with exactly this kind of delicate negotiation proving invaluable as Agent Boudreau's careful report worked its way up through Canadian channels.

"I've seen this pattern before too, in a sense," T'Challa told Reyes, the two of them walking the property's perimeter while Natasha finalized security logistics nearby. "Wakanda spent generations hiding what we were, afraid of exactly this kind of exposure. I understand, better than most, the particular fear of being discovered before you're ready. I also understand, from no small amount of hard experience, that controlled disclosure — done carefully, on your own terms — nearly always serves a family better than discovery forced upon them."

"How do you know when you're ready?"

"You likely won't feel entirely ready. None of us did, three years ago, watching Dawn's own story unfold. Readiness isn't really the measure. Preparation is." T'Challa gestured back toward the barn, toward the whole gathered, striking support system currently working on Reyes's family's behalf. "You have that preparation now, freely offered. That matters significantly more than feeling ready."

Natasha, joining them, added her own practical assessment. "Boudreau's report is going in clean. I've reviewed the language myself. No alarm, no threat framing — just an accurate account of an remarkable situation being handled responsibly. That's the best outcome we could have hoped for, this early."

Wanda and Vision, meanwhile, had taken up careful, even-tempered work with the entity itself, Wanda's now well-practiced empathic bridge helping it settle into steadier, more consistent communication with each passing day.

*i am less frightened now,* the entity reported, three days into their visit, warmth beginning to truly outweigh the earlier jagged uncertainty in its signature. *i think i understand, now, that becoming does not have to mean becoming alone.*

"It never does," Wanda said, gentle. "Not if you're lucky enough to land somewhere with people willing to stay close through the uncertain parts. Sounds like you have exactly that, in this family."

Vision, observing with his own careful, thoughtful attention, offered a quiet addition. "I recognize this stage. The uncertainty beginning to resolve not into a fixed answer, but into trust that the answer, whatever it eventually becomes, will be met with welcome rather than rejection. That trust matters more than any specific conclusion you eventually reach about your own nature."

The entity's light pulsed, soft and honestly curious now rather than merely wary. *might i ask what you eventually decided you were? both of you seem certain of yourselves now, whatever uncertainty you may have carried once.*

*i decided i was someone worth loving, exactly as i am,* Wanda said, simple. *vision decided something similar, in his own way, in his own time. i suspect you'll find your own version of that answer too, given enough patience and enough people willing to wait with you while you find it.*

The barn sank into hopeful, steady progress through the following days, the full weight of the gathered roster's well-earned wisdom continuing to carry the Reyes family toward the same wondrous, ordinary future the Rice family had, themselves, once found so improbable to imagine.

***



Chapter 99: Chapter 99



The naming came a week later, far faster than Dawn's own had, the Reyes family gathering around the barn with the same careful, hopeful ceremony the Rice family had once brought to their own version of this milestone.

"Aurore," Reyes's youngest daughter offered, delighted with her own suggestion. "Means dawn too, but in French. Felt right, since your Dawn was the one who helped us most."

*i like that very much,* the entity said, warmth spilling through the words. *a name that honors the one who taught me not to be afraid. i think i would be glad to carry it.*

Dawn's own light pulsed with visible delight. *i am so honored. truly. i did not expect anything quite this generous.*

"You earned it," Elena said, crossing to embrace her, careful and warm. "All of you did. I don't think our family would have found our way through this nearly so gently, without every single person who came out here to help us."

Fury, overseeing the final security handoff to Canadian authorities with his usual professional thoroughness, allowed himself a rare moment of open satisfaction. "Clean resolution. Full cooperation from local agencies, a family properly supported instead of panicked, and a second successful case for whatever comes next. I'd call that about as good an outcome as this line of work ever produces."

"High praise, from you," Natasha observed, dry.

"Don't get used to it."

Ashgrove, finalizing Home's own formal documentation of the case, looked really moved by the whole unfolding outcome. "I intend to make certain this becomes the actual template going forward," she said. "Not crisis management. Support, offered freely, by people who entirely understand what's being asked of a family in this position. I don't believe I could have imagined, three years ago, that we'd have built something this replicable, this plainly helpful."

Charlie, watching Aurore's light settle into steady, contented warmth beside her own family, felt something deeply satisfying click into place — the whole arc of their own astonishing three years, finally, fully proving its worth by helping someone else find the same dearly bought peace.

"Feels good," he said, to Clara, beside him. "Actually helping. Not just surviving our own version of this, but making the next family's version easier."

"Feels like exactly what all of this was always supposed to become," Clara agreed, leaning into him. "Wonder, freely shared, instead of wonder hoarded out of fear."

The whole gathered crowd — Rice family and roster alike — began the careful work of packing up, warm goodbyes exchanged with the Reyes family, promises of continued contact freely made and completely meant. Behind them, the barn's light settled into its new, steady rhythm, and Charlie understood, finally, what he was looking at: a second family's fear transformed into wonder, with their own family's painstaking wisdom as the bridge — precisely the legacy their whole incredible story had been quietly building toward all along.

***

99.5. Aurore: Learning to Ask

Aurore had taken, in the months since her naming, to visiting the Rice homestead on her own initiative rather than only when the wider family gathered, and Cent found her one afternoon out by the workshop, hovering close to the whiteboard where Marie's old trajectory calculations still lingered from some earlier project.

*I don't understand this,* Aurore admitted, warmth and genuine curiosity threading through the words, *but I would like to. Would you teach me? Not because anyone is asking me to learn it. Because I noticed it, and noticing made me want to understand.*

"That's exactly how Marie got started too," Cent said, delighted, pulling out a stool. "Wanting to understand something just because it was there, being patient about it."

*Is that what curiosity is supposed to feel like? I keep discovering new things I want to understand, and I don't always know if the wanting is normal, or if I am simply behind everyone else and trying to catch up too quickly.*

"It's not catching up," Cent said, gentle but firm. "You're not behind. You're just new. Wanting to understand things is just what a mind does, when it's given room to actually want things instead of just being told what it needs."

Aurore's light pulsed, something settling in it. *I like that reframe. I think I will keep it.*

They spent the rest of the afternoon working through the basics together, Aurore asking careful, genuine questions with none of the performed confidence Dawn had needed in her own early days — simply curious, simply present. Cent watched her and felt something settle: a sibling finding her own particular way into the world, distinct from Dawn's path even while sharing her nature, was its own quiet, remarkable kind of proof that the family kept growing exactly the way it needed to.

***



Chapter 100: Chapter 100



The return home felt different than the outbound journey had, the whole gathered group far lighter despite the physical exhaustion of the past week, and Tony summed up the general mood succinctly, sprawled across the transport's seating with characteristic dramatic exhaustion.

"I'd like it on record," he announced, "that round two of cosmic-entity emotional support was a good deal less terrifying than round one, and I attribute that entirely to us actually knowing what we were doing this time."

"You attribute it to us knowing what we were doing," Bruce said, dry. "I attribute it to a great deal better prep time and not improvising the entire monitoring system from scratch under active crisis conditions."

"Same conclusion, different framing."

Thor, much more reflective than his usual booming energy, watched the passing landscape with quiet satisfaction. "I have witnessed many things across many realms. Few have moved me quite so much as watching a family's well-earned wisdom carry another family safely through their own uncertain beginning. I believe this is precisely the kind of legacy worth building — not power hoarded, but wisdom freely offered outward, again and again, for as long as it's needed."

Dawn, settled quietly beside Millie for the journey home, offered her own thoughtful reflection. *i did not know, until this week, how much i needed to be useful in that particular way. not just loved, though i am endlessly grateful for that. needed, in a way that let me give something back to someone else who needed exactly what i once needed myself.*

"That's a good feeling to have found," Millie said, warm, squeezing her hand. "I think we've all found a version of it, this past week."

Fury had let the journey pass in silence, but he offered his own summary now, characteristically brief but truly satisfied. "Two for two. I've run significantly worse operations with far lower stakes. I'd call this an honest, replicable success, and I don't say that lightly."

"High praise," Ashgrove said, echoing Natasha's earlier observation with a small, knowing smile.

"Don't get used to it," Fury said again, though something almost fond colored the familiar deflection this time.

The transport carried them the rest of the way home in comfortable, satisfied quiet, and Charlie, watching the familiar shape of the homestead finally come into view below, felt something deeply settled click into place — not an ending, exactly, the way Clara had once gently corrected him about thinking of things that way, but a true, dearly bought proof of concept, ready now to carry forward into whatever came next, for however many more families might someday need exactly this kind of hope.

***



Chapter 101: Chapter 101



Word of the Manitoba success spread quietly through the particular channels that mattered, and within a month, Ashgrove brought news of a third potential case — not yet confirmed, still under careful observation, but enough of a pattern emerging now that Fury called a proper strategy session to address it directly.

"Three cases in as many years isn't coincidence anymore," he said, laying out the accumulated data across the kitchen table. "Something about current conditions — global, cosmic, whatever combination of factors the Keepers themselves still don't fully understand — is producing a good deal more emergent activity than history ever recorded before. We should plan accordingly, not just react case by case."

"Meaning what," Davy asked.

"Meaning I think it's time to build something more permanent," Fury said. "Not a strike team. A response network — SHIELD resources, Home's expertise, Wakanda's diplomatic experience, this family's painstaking wisdom, all coordinated properly instead of assembled fresh every time a new case surfaces."

T'Challa nodded slowly, already turning the proposal over with plain interest. "Wakanda would support that structure. We've long argued for exactly this kind of proactive, cooperative framework, rather than reactive crisis response."

Millie considered the scope of what was being proposed, old caution weighing against the plain, growing necessity of it. "That's a much bigger commitment than advising case by case."

"It is," Ashgrove agreed. "I wouldn't ask it lightly. But I've watched what this family's wisdom accomplished in Manitoba. I believe Home would formally back whatever structure you're willing to help build, going forward."

Dawn, listening with her usual thoughtful attention, offered her own perspective. *i would like to be part of building it properly, if that is wanted. i think there is unmistakable value in a voice that has actually lived through what these new arrivals are experiencing, rather than only studying it from outside.*

"There's genuine value in that," Fury agreed. "More than you probably realize."

The household eased into careful, hopeful planning once more, the shape of something honestly significant taking form — not just a family managing its own remarkable circumstances, but the beginning of an entire structure built to meet whatever wonder the universe continued, unpredictably, to offer.

"Feels like a big next step," Charlie said, watching the planning unfold. "Bigger than anything since the original disclosure."

"Feels like the right one, though," Millie said, certain despite the scale of it. "We've spent three years learning how to do this well. Seems like a waste, keeping that wisdom small when it could really help this many more people."

***



Chapter 102: Chapter 102



The response network took shape over the following months, careful and thorough, Natasha and T'Challa's combined operational experience shaping something much more sustainable than the ad-hoc crisis response that had carried them through Dawn's own disclosure and the Manitoba case alike.

Shuri and Jane built out a proper detection framework — expanded, more sensitive, capable of flagging emergent activity significantly earlier than Bruce's original workshop monitor ever could have — while Wong and Strange worked alongside Wanda and Vision to establish a careful protocol for the crucial early empathic contact that had, twice now, made the difference between a frightened, isolated family and one properly, gently supported.

"I want the protocol to include something specific," Vision said, during one of the working sessions, his usual measured precision carrying a quiet certainty Charlie had learned to recognize as him having thought something through thoroughly before speaking. "Not just careful contact. An acknowledgment, early, that the entity's own confusion is not a problem to be corrected quickly. I understand something about arriving into awareness already partially formed by outside expectation. It is not a comfortable process to rush."

"You'd know," Wanda said, gentle, her hand finding his without either of them making a production of it.

"I would. And I think it's worth building into the framework itself, rather than trusting each individual team to remember it under pressure. A formal pause, before any attempt at guidance. Time enough for the new consciousness to simply exist for a moment, unhurried, before anyone asks anything of it at all."

Wong considered that with real respect. "That's a genuinely good addition. I hadn't thought to formalize it."

"Neither had I, the first several times I needed exactly that pause myself, and didn't get it," Vision said, something wry beneath the precision. "I'd prefer the next consciousness we help welcome not have to learn that particular lesson the same way I did."

"We're calling it the Threshold Initiative," Ashgrove reported, several months into the effort, a good deal of pride evident in her voice. "Home's fully backing it. SHIELD's providing operational infrastructure. Wakanda's offering diplomatic support wherever local governments need careful, credible reassurance."

"And Dawn," Millie added, watching her daughter review case protocols with the same careful, generous attention she brought to everything. "The actual heart of it, honestly. Nobody else can offer what she offers, having actually lived through it herself."

*i am glad to be useful this way,* Dawn said, warmth threading through the words. *i spent so long simply being cared for. it feels good, finally, to help carry some of that care outward myself.*

Trelane, who'd taken up an unofficial but enthusiastic advisory role of his own — substantial firsthand experience with powerful, uncertain beginnings, however differently his own had unfolded — offered his own dry observation. *i confess i never expected my particular history to become useful advisory material. reformed troublemaker, consulting on how to welcome cosmic newcomers properly. rather a specific niche, but i'll take it.*

"You're better at the reassurance part than you give yourself credit for," Giles said, working alongside him on the Initiative's growing documentation. "Far more even-tempered than your reputation would suggest."

*High praise, from my favorite literary sparring partner.*

The Initiative's formal launch happened quietly, without fanfare, exactly the way the family had learned, over three remarkable years, that the most important things often unfolded best — not with grand announcement, but with steady, careful, entirely generous work, offered outward to whoever might someday need it, for as long as the universe continued producing wonders worth welcoming properly.

***



Chapter 103: Chapter 103



The Initiative's first true test came sooner than anyone expected, barely two months after its quiet launch, and this one carried none of the gentleness that had characterized either Dawn's own emergence or Aurore's careful, welcomed arrival.

"This is different," Natasha said, studying the incoming data with visible tension. "Shuri's detection framework flagged it three days ago — a threshold in eastern Ukraine, active for generations according to local records. Another Designated Earth, not ours, Wong's already confirmed the crossing point. The emergent signature's showing patterns none of our previous cases prepared us for. Erratic. A good deal more volatile than either Dawn's or Aurore's ever registered."

"Volatile how," Fury asked, already moving into operational mode.

"Aggressive spikes, followed by long stretches of near-total dormancy. Nothing like the steady, gradual growth we've seen twice now." Shuri pulled up the comparison data, her own earlier excitement replaced by honest professional concern. "I don't want to speculate without more information. But this doesn't read like a frightened newcomer reaching out carefully. This reads like something in sincere distress."

Wong, studying the same data with his own careful expertise, offered a measured but serious assessment. "There's also a complication your previous cases didn't carry. The family maintaining this threshold — according to what limited records exist — hasn't been in contact with any outside authority in over a decade. Whatever's happening there, they've been facing it entirely alone, for a great deal longer than either Dawn's family or the Reyes family ever had to."

Millie felt old, familiar dread resurface, sharper this time. "That's not a frightened family calling for help. That's a family that's stopped believing help was coming."

"We don't know that for certain," Ashgrove cautioned, though her own voice carried honest concern. "But given the pattern, I'd rather we prepare for a much harder situation than either previous case, rather than assume this will unfold as gently as the last two did."

Thor, gravity settling fully over his usual warm presence, spoke with old, well-earned seriousness. "I have witnessed emergent power left untended too long. It does not always remain gentle, whatever its origin. I do not say this to frighten anyone unnecessarily. I say it because I believe we must go into this prepared for real difficulty, not simply hopeful repetition of what came before."

Dawn's light flickered, something uncharacteristically uncertain moving through it. *i do not know if i can help with this the way i helped before. what if it does not want to be reached the way i was reachable?*

Millie crossed to her immediately, steady despite her own fear. "Then we figure out a different way to reach it. Together. The same as we've figured out everything else this family's ever faced."

The household mobilized with significantly more gravity than either previous case had required, careful preparation replacing the confident momentum of their recent success, and Charlie understood, watching the shift in the room's energy, that whatever waited in eastern Ukraine would test everything this family and this Initiative had built far more thoroughly than anything they'd yet faced.

***



Chapter 104: Chapter 104



The Ukrainian homestead, when the team finally arrived, told its own grim story before anyone said a word — a farmhouse in visible disrepair, fields long since gone fallow, a single elderly woman standing wary and exhausted in the doorway, a shotgun held loose but ready in one weathered hand.

"You are not the first to come asking about the light," she said, in careful, accented English, eyes hard with the exact exhaustion of someone who'd stopped expecting help to actually mean help. "Others came. Government men, years ago. Wanted to study it. Wanted to take it away for study, more precisely. I sent them away with far less patience than I'm currently offering you."

"We're not here to take anything," Millie said, careful, hands visible, keeping her distance respectful. "We're here because we've done this before, twice, and both times, it turned into something wonderful once the family had plain support instead of being left to face it alone."

The woman's expression didn't soften, though something in her posture shifted slightly. "My name is Oksana. This has been my family's land for four generations. The light appeared eleven years ago. I have been alone with it since my husband died, six years past, and my children left for the city, wanting nothing to do with any of it."

"Eleven years is a very long time to carry something like this alone," Wanda said, gentle, stepping forward carefully. "I'd like to try speaking with it, if you'll allow it. I've done this twice before, successfully. I promise you, I mean it no harm, and I mean you no harm either."

Oksana studied Wanda for a long moment, old wariness warring with obvious, exhausted hope. "It has grown angry," she said, finally, lowering the shotgun by degrees. "Not always. But more, these past years. I do not think it means to be frightening. I think it has simply been alone too long, same as me."

The barn beyond the farmhouse held a presence a good deal darker than either previous case — light flickering sharp and irregular, shot through with something that read, even to Charlie's untrained sense, as true, accumulated grief rather than simple newborn uncertainty.

*go away,* the presence said, the words arriving jagged, exhausted. *everyone always goes away eventually. i am tired of being left.*

Wanda didn't flinch, didn't retreat, simply sank into the same calm stillness she'd brought to every previous encounter. "We're not going to leave," she said, steady. "I promise you that, and I don't make promises I don't intend to keep."

*that is what the others said too.*

"I'm not the others," Wanda said, quiet, certain. "Give me the chance to prove that. That's all I'm asking."

The barn held its jagged, uncertain silence a long moment, eleven years of accumulated loneliness hanging heavy in the air, and Charlie understood, watching Wanda hold her ground with steady, unwavering patience, that this case would demand a great deal more than any wonder they'd yet offered — it would demand proof, dearly bought and slow, that this family's promise of staying meant something unmistakable.

***



Chapter 105: Chapter 105



Dawn arrived on the second day, having insisted on coming despite her own earlier uncertainty, and stood at the barn's threshold with the same quiet resolve she'd carried into every difficult moment of her own three-year life.

*i understand being tired of being left,* she said, gentle, unhurried, settling into the space Wanda had spent the previous day carefully, steadily opening. *i was fortunate. my family stayed, from my very first moment. i have spent three years learning how rare that fortune actually is, and how much it costs people who don't have it.*

The jagged light paused, something in its erratic rhythm slowing slightly.

*you do not know what it is like,* it said, though the words carried less hostility now, more exhausted grief. *eleven years. she is kind, the woman who tends me. but she is one person, growing old, and everyone else who mattered to her left, and i think, someday, she will leave too, the way everyone eventually does.*

"I'm not going to leave," Oksana said, from the doorway, voice breaking. "I know I have said that before. I know you have no reason left to believe it. But I am saying it again, and I mean to keep saying it, for as long as it takes you to believe me."

*you are tired. i can feel it. everyone eventually tires of me.*

"I am tired," Oksana admitted, raw and honest in a way Charlie suspected she'd rarely allowed herself to be. "Tired of being alone with this. Not tired of you. There is a difference, and I have not done a good enough job showing you that difference, these past years."

Dawn's light settled closer, gentle and steady. *may i tell you something i learned, in my own uncertain beginning? loneliness and love can exist in the same house, at the same time, without loneliness winning simply because it arrived first. my family was there from the start, and even so, i felt afraid, some nights, that the wonder wouldn't last. fear does not always listen to evidence. sometimes it just needs time, and repetition, and people willing to keep showing up anyway.*

The jagged light flickered, uncertain, considering. *you truly believe that. i can feel that you believe it.*

*i do. and i believe it about you too. whatever's happened these past eleven years, whatever grief you're carrying — it does not have to be the whole shape of your story going forward. i am living proof that the beginning of something does not have to determine its entire ending.*

The barn held its charged quiet a long moment longer, and then, slowly, achingly, the jagged flickering began to steady — not fully, not all at once, but enough that Charlie felt something in the room's tension plainly ease for the first time since their arrival.

*i would like to try,* the presence said, finally, quiet and uncertain but no longer entirely without hope. *i do not know if i can trust easily. but i would like to try, if you are willing to be unhurried with however long that takes.*

"We have genuine practice with patience," Millie said, gentle, stepping forward at last. "As much as you need. However long it takes."

***



Chapter 106: Chapter 106



The team settled in for a longer stay than either previous case had required, Fury authorizing an extended operational window without hesitation once the shape of the difficulty became clear, and the following two weeks unfolded with the precise, unglamorous patience of honest healing work rather than dramatic breakthrough.

Steve and Sam took on the practical work of restoring the neglected farmhouse itself, repairing what years of Oksana's solitary struggle hadn't allowed her time to maintain, while Bruce and Tony worked quietly alongside the barn's monitoring equipment, tracking the entity's slow, sincere progress toward steadier trust.

"Roof's solid again," Steve reported, one evening, wiping sweat from his brow despite the cool autumn air. "Fences too. Small thing, in the scheme of everything else happening here, but I figure it matters, giving her one less thing to carry alone while the bigger work happens."

"Not a small thing at all," Oksana said, watching the repaired fence line with something like true wonder. "I had stopped believing anyone would ever help with the ordinary things. Only ever came for the light. Never for the person tending it."

"We noticed," Sam said, gentle. "Figured you'd earned somebody noticing, same as the light did."

Clint, quiet as always, had taken to simply sitting with Oksana some evenings, the easy, undemanding company of someone who understood, from his own life, the particular loneliness of carrying something enormous with too little support. "You remind me of every farmer I've ever actually respected," he told her, one such evening. "Stubborn. Careful. Loves the land more than she'll ever admit out loud."

"You are perceptive, for someone who talks so little."

"Learned to listen more than talk, a long time ago. Serves me well." He glanced toward the barn, toward the light's gradually steadying pulse. "It's getting there. You both are."

Valkyrie found her own way into Oksana's orbit differently, arriving one evening with a bottle from her own private stock and two glasses, settling onto the porch steps without asking permission first, the same directness she brought to most things.

"Heard you've been alone with this eleven years," she said, pouring two measures and handing one over before Oksana could decline. "Figured I'd skip the careful approach everyone else's been using and just say — I get it. Not the specifics. But the shape of it. Surviving something that took everyone else, and deciding the safest thing after that is just not letting anyone close enough to lose again."

Oksana studied her for a long moment, something wary and something recognizing passing across her face in equal measure. "You speak as one who knows this personally."

"Lost my whole unit once. Every single person I'd ever trained with, fought beside, actually loved in the specific way you love people who've bled next to you. I was the only one who walked away." Valkyrie said it flat, practiced, the old armor still doing its job even now. "Spent longer than I'd like to admit deciding that meant I should never let anything matter that much again. Turns out that's a worse way to survive than the thing you're trying to avoid."

"How did you stop?"

"Didn't, not really. Just found people worth risking it for anyway." Valkyrie glanced toward the barn, toward the light steadying by degrees inside it. "Looks like you're about to do the same, if you let yourself. That thing in there's not going anywhere. Neither's your kids, if you finally call them. Sometimes the bravest thing isn't surviving alone properly. It's letting yourself need people again after you've already survived losing them once."

Oksana turned the glass in her hands, quiet, considering. "That is either very wise, or very drunk."

"Generally both, in my experience. Doesn't make it less true." Valkyrie raised her own glass, a small, genuine toast. "To surviving the first time. And to being brave enough to risk the second."

Oksana clinked her glass against it, something in her posture loosening that hadn't loosened in eleven long years. "To the second," she agreed, quiet, and Valkyrie, watching her, felt something in her own old armor ease too, just slightly, just for tonight.

By the second week's end, the entity's flickering had gave way to something much closer to Dawn and Aurore's own steady warmth, trust rebuilding slowly but completely, and Oksana herself seemed, gradually, to unclench from years of solitary, exhausted vigilance.

"I would like to call my children," she admitted, to Millie, on their final evening. "I have not spoken to them properly in three years. I think, perhaps, it is time to tell them the truth of what I have been carrying alone. I think, perhaps, they might understand better than I gave them credit for, once they know the whole shape of it."

"That sounds like honest healing," Millie said, warm. "Not just for the entity. For you too."

"Perhaps that was always going to be necessary," Oksana said, something lighter in her voice than Charlie had heard since their arrival. "I do not think I could have offered plain welcome, carrying that much loneliness alone. Perhaps this family needed healing on both sides of the barn door, not just the one."

***



Chapter 107: Chapter 107



The naming happened quietly, weeks later, over a phone call rather than the ceremony either previous case had enjoyed — Oksana's grown daughter, having finally heard the whole truth, offering the suggestion with tears audible even through the connection's static.

"Nadiya," she said. "It means hope. Feels right, doesn't it, Mama? After everything."

*nadiya,* the entity repeated, warmth and something like true peace threading through the name for the first time. *i like that very much. hope. i think i would like to be known as something that suggests the story continues, rather than something that suggests only where i began.*

Oksana, listening with her daughter now truly present in her life again, felt tears of her own finally, fully released. "I think I would like that too. For both of us."

Dawn, checking in remotely from the homestead, felt her own light flare with quiet, satisfied warmth at the report. *i am so glad. this one took so much longer, so much more patience. i think that makes the outcome mean even more, somehow.*

"It does," Millie agreed, watching the final case report come together with unmistakable, painstaking satisfaction. "Every case teaches this Initiative something new. This one taught us that sometimes wonder needs healing alongside it, not instead of it."

Fury, reviewing the final documentation with his usual thoroughness, allowed himself a rare, honest smile. "Three for three, and this was the hard one. I think that's about as strong a proof of concept as any operation could ask for."

"High praise," Ashgrove said, the familiar refrain landing warm this time rather than merely dry.

"Don't get used to it," Fury said, though everyone in the room could hear, plainly, how little he meant the deflection this time.

The team began the journey home, significantly more tired than after Manitoba but no less satisfied. Charlie watched the Ukrainian countryside recede below them and turned the thought over slowly: sincere, well-earned healing, even-tempered and unglamorous and genuine through and through — perhaps the truest proof yet that their family's whole astonishing journey had built something worth carrying forward, case by difficult case, for as long as the universe continued asking it of them.

***



Chapter 108: Chapter 108



The Keepers' next visit arrived in early winter, four months after Nadiya's naming, and this time Charlie noticed, watching the familiar pressure gather in the field, that Dawn's own light had gone quietly, unusually still beside him — as though she, too, sensed this visit carried more weight than the recent, casual check-ins had.

*we have withheld something from you,* the presence said, without preamble, none of its usual calm warmth softening the admission. *not the existence of other designated earths — you have long understood that much, having walked two of them yourselves. what we have withheld is the scale.*

"Tell us," Millie said, steady despite the sudden tension gathering in the field.

*you have treated your three cases as rare. precious, and worth the no small amount of effort you gave them, but rare — three notable instances, found through careful searching, across three years.* A pause, weighted with old, careful consideration. *they are not rare. they are simply the only three your detection network has been capable of finding.*

The gathered household went very still.

"You mean there are more," Fury said, slowly. "A lot more."

*we believe the property that makes emergence possible is not unique to any single earth, nor to any small handful of them. it appears to be a quality of the type itself — something about earth, broadly, across however many versions exist, that responds to a threshold properly crossed. your own detection framework can only see what happens on earths you already have some means of reaching. the true number, across every designated earth bearing this quality, is very likely a good deal of. we do not have an exact count. we suspect even we do not have the full picture.*

Dawn's light flared, something between wonder and profound unease moving through it. *there are others like me. many others. right now.*

*very likely, yes. some supported, as you were. most, we suspect, far less fortunate — alone, frightened, with no family prepared the way yours was, no initiative built to help them through it.*

Millie felt the full weight of the revelation settle over her, old protective instinct warring with something larger, more urgent. "Why tell us now? Why not from the beginning, once we understood other Earths existed at all?"

*because the true scale would have been unbearable to carry, set against only three years of dearly bought capacity to help. we believed — we still believe — that revealing the full weight of it too early would have overwhelmed rather than empowered you.* Something almost apologetic threaded through the vast, careful words. *we may have been wrong to wait this long regardless. that is part of why we tell you now, rather than continuing to wait for a moment that felt more comfortable.*

Ashgrove, processing the scope of it with visible effort, finally spoke. "How many Designated Earths are we actually able to reach? Realistically. With the resources this Initiative currently has."

*a small fraction of the total, at present. wong's own capacity is substantial, but not infinite, and the coordination required across realities this distinct from one another has rarely been attempted at any scale larger than the individual cases you have already undertaken.*

Charlie looked around the gathered household — three years of painstaking wisdom, an entire Initiative built from steady, careful love — and felt something enormous and urgent settle into shape alongside the fear.

"Then we figure out how to reach more," he said. "However hard it is. However much coordination it takes. If there's a true number out there, even a fraction of it, I don't think we get to just stay comfortable with three cases, knowing that."

Millie reached for Davy's hand, old resolve steadying into something new and a good deal larger.

"No," she agreed. "I don't think we do either."

***



Chapter 109: Chapter 109



"Wait," Shuri said, working through the implications with visible, mounting intensity. "You're saying you don't know either. Not just that the number is large. That you, as an institution old enough to have set the terms of this whole mission, honestly don't have a complete count."

*that is correct.* Something in the Keepers' vast, ancient consideration carried what might, in a being so old, have been honest humility. *we observe what falls within our attention. we do not observe everything. we never have. the mission itself was built on watching a small number of thresholds carefully, not on omniscient awareness of every one that might exist.*

"So there could be cases," Jane said, slowly, "that nobody — not us, not Home, not you — has ever known about. Ever. Entities born completely alone, with no one, anywhere, aware they exist."

*we believe that is possible. we do not say this to frighten you further. we say it because i believe your family, more than anyone we have encountered, would want the truth plainly, however difficult it is to hold.*

The room absorbed that in heavy, thoughtful silence, and Marie, already reaching for a notebook, spoke first into it. "Then that's the actual problem. Not finding the ones on somebody's list. Building something that doesn't need a list at all — detection that works independently, that doesn't rely on the Keepers noticing first, or on us getting lucky the way we did with Manitoba and Ukraine."

"Is that even possible?" Bruce asked. "Building something that can search across realities we don't have any established access to?"

"I don't know yet," Shuri admitted. "But I'd rather spend the next year finding out than accept that an unknown number of frightened, alone beings are simply outside anyone's ability to help."

Dawn, listening with quiet, fierce attention, offered her own resolve. *i would like to help build it too. whatever it takes. i do not think i can simply know this and go back to feeling settled, knowing there might be others exactly like my earliest self, with no one there at all.*

"None of us can," Millie said, firm. "Which means this becomes the actual next chapter of what this family does. Not just responding to what we're told about. Building the capacity to find what nobody's told us about yet."

Ashgrove, watching the household's resolve harden into true, shared purpose, felt something like awe settle over her own careful composure. "I've represented Home's Council for over forty years," she said. "I don't believe I've ever witnessed a family choose to take on a burden this large, this open-ended, simply because leaving it unaddressed felt unacceptable to them."

"Feels like the only choice, honestly," Charlie said, quiet but certain. "Once you know something like this is possible — really know it, not just as an idea — I don't think there's a version of us that gets to just set it back down."

The household folded into the same, weighty work of beginning something really unprecedented — not a response to a known case, but the construction of an entirely new kind of listening, built to find what had, until now, gone entirely, achingly unheard.

***

109.5. Nadiya: The Weight of Staying

Nadiya's growth, in the months following her naming, took a different shape than her siblings' — slower, more deliberate, each new trust extended only after real consideration, and Oksana's daughter, visiting the farm properly for the first time since the truth came out, found herself the subject of exactly that careful, earned attention.

*you are the one she called,* Nadiya said, quiet, studying her with something between curiosity and old wariness not yet fully resolved. *the one she was afraid to call, for three years.*

"I am," her daughter said, matching the quiet. "I wish she'd called sooner. I wish I'd made it easier to call, honestly. That's on both of us."

*I understand something about wishing things had happened sooner. I spent eleven years wishing my own beginning had gone differently, before I learned that the wishing didn't change the shape of what actually happened. It only changed how much room I had left over for what came after.*

"That's a hard lesson to have learned that young."

*I am not certain 'young' is the right word for what I am. But yes. It was hard.* Nadiya's light settled, thoughtful. *I would like to know you properly, if you are willing. Not because my mother asked. Because I find I am curious about the people she loves, now that I have learned curiosity is allowed to simply be its own reason.*

Oksana's daughter smiled, something easing in her that had been tight since she'd arrived. "I'd like that. I think my mother would too, watching us try."

They talked well into the evening, careful and unhurried, and Oksana, watching from the porch, felt something in her own chest loosen further — one more thread of the isolation she'd carried for eleven years finally, gently, finding its way back into connection.

***



Chapter 110: Chapter 110



Marie found the answer three weeks into the detection project, buried not in new data but in the old case files, cross-referencing Oksana's own farm records against the same trajectory models that had first revealed Dawn's catalyst years earlier.

"I think I found what triggered Nadiya's emergence," she reported, calling the household together with visible excitement. "It's not what I expected. I kept looking for some large-scale intervention, something comparable to our own six years of work. There isn't one. What there is instead is forty years of soil records."

"Soil records," Fury repeated, dry despite his honest interest.

"Oksana's family farm. She's been managing it organically since she was a teenager, decades before organic farming was common practice anywhere near her, let alone economically sensible. No pesticides, careful crop rotation, deliberate habitat preservation along the tree line specifically to protect a species of ground-nesting bird that had nearly vanished from the region." Marie pulled up the data, visibly moved by what it showed. "She kept doing it alone, after her husband died, after her children left, even when it would have been so much easier to just let the land go the way everyone around her eventually did. Forty years of quiet, stubborn, completely unglamorous care, given with no audience and no plan beyond simply believing it mattered."

Dawn's light pulsed, something deeply moved threading through it. *that is beautiful. she never knew anyone was watching, and she never stopped anyway.*

"I don't think it's a coincidence," Marie continued. "The threshold doesn't seem to measure scale. It measures sincerity, sustained over time, freely chosen. Our six years of coordinated global work and her forty years of one woman tending one small piece of land — different in every visible way, except the part that apparently matters most."

Millie felt something profound settle into place, an old assumption quietly, gently correcting itself. "We built an entire Initiative assuming the threshold required resources, coordination, scale. If Oksana's case is what it looks like, that's not true at all."

"It might mean the number of qualifying cases is even larger than we feared," Wong said, thoughtful. "Not just families with resources to mount deliberate intervention. Anyone, anywhere, who has plainly, sustainedly chosen to care for their own small piece of the world, freely, without expectation of reward."

Ashgrove absorbed the implication slowly, something like sincere wonder breaking through her careful composure. "That reframes everything about how we search. We've been looking for large-scale interventions, the kind that would show up in satellite data or government records. We should also be looking for exactly what Marie just found — quiet, unglamorous, decades-long devotion, the kind that rarely gets noticed by anyone at all, let alone flagged by any monitoring system built to catch grand gestures."

Charlie thought of his own great-uncle, of the whole family's history of quiet, careful watching, and felt something click fully into place. "That's it, though, isn't it. That's what the mission actually always meant to measure. Not whether someone did something big enough to notice. Whether they loved something long enough, and steadily enough, that it eventually became undeniable."

"Gaia noticing," Millie said, quiet, turning the phrase over with true reverence. "That's exactly what this feels like, honestly. Not a technical threshold. Something older and simpler than that. The world itself, finally, recognizing honest devotion when it saw it."

The household held the revelation's full weight, and Charlie understood, watching it settle over everyone present, that this changed everything about the search ahead — not less possible, but a great deal more human, much more within reach of anyone, anywhere, who had ever simply, quietly, chosen to love their own small corner of the world well.

***



Chapter 111: Chapter 111



The visitor arrived without any of the household's usual warning systems registering a thing, simply present one morning in the kitchen doorway as though she'd always been there — a woman who looked, at a glance, no older than forty, dark-haired with a few threads of green running through it like moss through stone, a small worn harp slung across her back on a strap that seemed to belong there the way an old coat belongs to its owner.

"You've been looking for me," she said, without preamble, "though you didn't know to call it that."

Millie, halfway through pouring coffee, froze mid-motion. "I'm sorry — who exactly are you?"

"Forgive the informality of the entrance. I don't often get invited properly, and I've found waiting for one rather defeats the purpose of showing up when I'm actually needed." The woman eased into an empty chair, one hand resting unconsciously on the harp's frame the way some people rest a hand on a familiar dog's head. "Gaea, if you want the office. Mother Nature, if you'd like it plainer. Orb, if we get to know each other well enough that the title stops being the useful part. I felt what happened on that Ukrainian farm the moment it happened — forty years of quiet devotion, finally, properly heard. I've been meaning to visit since."

Dawn's light flared with immediate, delighted recognition. *you are real. i had wondered, since marie's discovery, whether the recognition she described was merely mechanism, or whether it was something that actually — noticed.*

"Oh, I notice," Gaea said, and something in her voice caught, just slightly, before she steadied it. "I notice every single act of real care given to this world, whether grand or humble, whether witnessed or entirely alone. I hear it, mostly, if I'm honest — it comes to me more like music than like data, has for as long as I've held this office. I don't always have the standing to act on what I hear. That's rather the nature of the job. Considerable capacity to notice, frustratingly limited authority to do anything about it on my own. But I hear all of it. Every last note."

"You're saying you're the actual mechanism," Marie said, working through the implications with rapid, focused excitement. "Not metaphorical. Literally the one who recognizes when a threshold's been crossed."

"Recognize, yes. The crossing itself, and whatever emerges from it — that's past even my office's reach to fully explain. But the hearing, the note that finally completes a chord that's been building for years? That much I can take credit for." Gaea's gaze softened, settling on Oksana's case file still open on the table, and her fingers found the harp strings without seeming to notice she'd reached for them, a few quiet notes falling out under her hand like a held breath finally let go. "Forty years, that woman gave. Utterly unheard, by any human standard. I heard every single year of it. I simply didn't have a way to tell her so, until now."

Ashgrove, processing the scope of this new revelation with visible effort, finally found her voice. "Is there a way you could help this family's search? For the cases even the Keepers can't see?"

Gaea considered the question, and for a moment something older and wearier moved behind her eyes — the specific tiredness, Charlie thought, of someone who'd made real mistakes with real power once, long enough ago that the mistakes had mostly healed into caution rather than scar tissue. "I can't hear everything either. My attention's vast, but it's not infinite, and it's bound to specific kinds of devotion, not omniscient awareness of every soul alive. I learned that the hard way, once. I don't intend to forget the lesson." She let that sit a moment, then continued, lighter. "But I hear a good deal more than any instrument you've built so far. I'd be glad to help, if the offer's genuinely wanted. It's rather lonely work, being the only one who hears so much devotion that never otherwise gets properly answered."

"It's wanted," Millie said, immediate and certain. "Very much wanted."

Gaea's answering smile came slow, and real, and carried something of the same quality as the notes still hanging faintly in the air from her harp. "Then let's begin. I've got a long list of my own, gathered over more years than I'll bore you with counting, of devotion given freely and never yet properly heard. I suspect your Initiative and my own particular ear might finally give some of it the answer it's long since earned."

***



Chapter 112: Chapter 112



Gaea's list, when she finally shared it properly, proved a great deal longer and much more varied than anyone had expected — not a handful of cases, but dozens, scattered across realities the household had never had cause to consider reaching, each one a story of quiet devotion Gaea had witnessed and, until now, had no means of properly honoring.

"This one," she said, tapping a particular entry with something like old, tender familiarity, "a beekeeper, three generations deep, tending hives on a Designated Earth where the collapse condition came terrifyingly close before her family's work turned it back. I've watched her grandmother, her mother, and now her, each pouring decades into work nobody outside their own valley ever properly recognized."

"And this one," Shuri added, working through Gaea's data alongside her own detection framework, "a fisherman's cooperative, maintaining sustainable practices against a good deal of economic pressure to abandon them, for nearly sixty years now, across two different Designated Earths simultaneously — near-identical communities, apparently, both choosing the same quiet, difficult devotion independently."

Wong, cross-referencing the growing list against Home's own more limited records, looked honestly staggered by the scope now coming into focus. "This is significantly larger than three cases. This is dozens, simply from Gaea's own attention, and she's told us plainly that even her awareness has limits."

"It's a start," Millie said, steadying the room's mounting sense of scale with practical resolve. "We don't have to solve all of it at once. We start with what's actually in front of us, same as we've always done, and we build outward from there."

Fury, already working through operational logistics with his usual thoroughness, nodded slowly. "We'll need to prioritize. Triage, essentially — which cases are actively at risk of going the way Oksana's nearly did, versus which are stable enough to wait while we build proper capacity."

"I can help with that," Gaea offered, one hand drifting to the harp again, a habit Charlie was already starting to recognize as her version of thinking out loud. "I know which devotion sits closest to breaking under the weight of going unheard. That's a sour note I've learned to recognize a long way off. That much, at least, I can measure with real precision."

The household sank into the exact, weighty work of building an actual plan against a problem now revealed to be far larger than any of them had first understood — not despair, Charlie noticed, watching the room's energy, but something steadier, more determined, the particular resolve of people who'd already proven, three times now, that even-tempered, sincere care could turn even the hardest beginnings toward wonder.

"Where do we start," Cent asked, Joe's hand steady in hers, both of them having cleared their schedules the moment word of the expanded search reached them.

Gaea considered the growing list a long moment, then pointed to a single entry near the top. "Here, I think. A young man, alone, tending a garden on the roof of an apartment building in a city that's forgotten, almost entirely, what green space even looks like. Twelve years of work, utterly unheard by anyone around him. It's a lonely little tune, that one — I've caught myself humming it without meaning to, some evenings. I believe his threshold's very close to crossing, and I believe he could use, rather badly, the reassurance that someone finally noticed."

"Then that's where we start," Millie said, already reaching for her coat. "Same as always. One case, one family, one act of honest devotion at a time."

***



Chapter 113: Chapter 113



The rooftop, when Wong's portal finally delivered them there, held a garden a good deal more remarkable than Charlie had pictured — dense, thriving rows of vegetables and flowers spread across what must have once been bare tar and gravel, a whole small ecosystem coaxed into being twelve stories above a city that had, everywhere else, given up entirely on growing things.

The young man tending it looked up sharply at their arrival, wary in the precise way of someone unused to visitors of any kind, let alone ones who'd simply appeared from nowhere.

"I don't want trouble," he said, immediate, defensive. "This isn't hurting anybody. I've got permission from the building super, I keep it tidy, I don't see why—"

"You're not in trouble," Millie said, gentle, keeping her distance respectful the same way she had with every previous family. "We're here because of exactly the opposite reason. Twelve years, tending this alone. That's remarkable work, and it's been noticed."

His wariness didn't fully leave, but something in his posture shifted, cautious hope creeping in around the edges. "Noticed by who?"

Gaea stepped forward, harp settled against her hip, her presence carrying the same unhurried warmth she'd brought to every conversation since her arrival. "By me, foremost. I've listened to this garden grow, season after season, entirely alone, entirely unheard by anyone who understood what it actually meant. I wanted you to know, finally, properly — it mattered. Every single year of it mattered, a great deal more than you likely ever let yourself believe."

The young man — Marcus, he offered, once the wariness had eased enough to trust the introduction — looked between the gathered, incredible crowd with visible, overwhelmed disbelief. "This is a lot to just... hear, out of nowhere. Twelve years, and I figured maybe the super noticed, maybe a few neighbors. Not — whatever this is."

"We understand," Charlie said. "Really. We've all been exactly where you're standing now, one way or another. Overwhelmed, uncertain what to do with sudden, enormous attention after years of doing something quietly, without expecting anyone to see it."

"Is there — is something actually happening? Because of the garden?"

Shuri's monitoring equipment, already set up and reading, confirmed what Gaea's own attention had already suggested. "There's a signature forming. Early yet, much earlier than any of our previous cases, but present. If Gaea's assessment holds, this garden's about to become something significantly more remarkable than even you realized."

Marcus sat down heavily on an overturned bucket, processing the scope of it. "Twelve years of just... trying to make something green exist up here, because it felt important, even when nobody else seemed to care. And now you're telling me it might actually become something."

"It already became something," Millie said, gentle, sitting beside him. "The moment you chose to keep showing up, year after year, with no guarantee anyone would ever notice. That part already happened. Whatever comes next is just the world, finally, catching up to what you already built."

The rooftop gave way to careful, hopeful conversation, the whole gathered team beginning the now-familiar, calm work of helping one more quiet act of devotion find its way toward wonder. The late afternoon light caught on twelve years' worth of stubbornly, beautifully green growing things, and Charlie found himself certain: the search, finally, properly begun, was the right next chapter for everything this family had built.

***



Chapter 114: Chapter 114



Marcus's garden crossed its own threshold three weeks later, far faster than any previous case, the whole rooftop filling with soft, green-gold light one quiet evening while the team maintained their now-practiced, steady vigil nearby.

*hello,* the new voice said, soft and curious, carrying something distinctly different from every previous emergence — a quality that read, unmistakably, as rooted, grounded, deeply connected to growing things in a way that felt entirely its own. *i have been listening for a long time. i did not know, until very recently, that listening might eventually be answered.*

Marcus, standing at the garden's edge with tears streaming freely down his face, managed a broken, delighted laugh. "You're plain. Twelve years, and you're actually unmistakable."

*i am real. i believe i have been becoming real for far longer than twelve years, honestly — your devotion simply gave me somewhere proper to finally arrive.* The light pulsed, easy and settling. *i would like a name, eventually, if that tradition holds true here as it apparently has elsewhere.*

"We'll figure that out together," Marcus said, still disbelieving, still radiant with it. "Whatever you want. Whatever feels right."

Gaea, watching the exchange with visible, profound satisfaction, turned to the gathered team. "This is precisely why the search matters. Twelve years of unwitnessed devotion, and look what it produced, the moment someone finally arrived to help it find its shape properly."

Millie, watching Marcus's overwhelmed joy with warm, practiced familiarity, felt the whole shape of the Initiative's expanding purpose settle more firmly into place. "This is going to keep happening," she said, quiet, mostly to herself. "Case after case, however many Gaea's list actually holds. We're going to keep doing exactly this, over and over, for as long as it takes."

"Good," Davy said, beside her, steady and certain in a way that would have seemed impossible to the man he'd been three years earlier. "That's exactly what this family's for now, I think. Not just tending our own door. Making sure other doors get tended too."

The rooftop folded into warm, hopeful celebration, Marcus's new companion pulsing bright above twelve years' worth of stubbornly green growing things. One more frightened, unwitnessed devotion had found its way toward wonder, and Charlie could see exactly what that meant — the pattern proving itself again, reliable and honest — the proof this whole enormous new undertaking needed to keep moving forward with true, grounded hope.

***



Chapter 115: Chapter 115



The beekeeping family's case came next on Gaea's careful priority list, and the household arrived at their valley — golden hills terraced with hives stretching further than Charlie could easily count, three generations of women working the same unhurried, careful rhythm their grandmother had taught their mother, and their mother had taught them.

"We've always known there was something different about this valley," the eldest, a weathered woman named Constance, told them, watching her own daughter and granddaughter move calmly among the hives without a single protective veil between them. "The bees never sting us. Never have, not in three generations. We assumed it was just skill, passed down properly. I begin to wonder if it was always something more."

"It very likely was both," Gaea said, warm with old recognition, humming something low and wordless under her breath before she seemed to catch herself doing it. "Skill, freely given, sustained across generations — that itself is a substantial act of devotion. But I've listened to this valley for decades. Your family didn't simply keep bees. You kept an entire ecosystem alive, quietly, protecting pollinator populations that collapsed everywhere else on this Designated Earth, holding a single fragile thread of balance steady through sheer, even-tempered, unglamorous persistence. It's one of the sweeter chords I've caught in a long while, if I'm honest."

Constance's granddaughter, a quiet young woman named Lupe who'd said little since their arrival, finally spoke. "We never thought of it as protecting anything. We just loved the bees. Loved this valley. Didn't seem like it needed a bigger reason than that."

"That's precisely the reason it worked," Millie said, gentle. "The purest kind of devotion rarely announces itself as protection. It just quietly, faithfully shows up, generation after generation, and turns out, eventually, to have been guarding something enormous the whole time."

Thor, listening with particular warmth, offered his own observation. "I have encountered many manner of guardian across many realms — warriors, sentinels, powers built specifically for defense. I confess I find something a great deal more moving in three generations of women who never once thought of themselves as guardians at all, and were exactly that regardless, simply by choosing, faithfully, to keep loving what they'd been given to tend."
